Abstract
This note is the technical record of a methods and benchmark study of unbinned unfolding with
OmniFold on neutrino data. MINERvA medium-energy open data are unfolded without binning,
simultaneously in five observables: muon transverse and longitudinal momenta, available hadronic
energy, three-momentum transfer and hadronic invariant mass. The unfolding reproduces the
published muon-kinematic cross-section integral within about one percent, with a correlated
shape difference whose source has not been identified. A binned iterative Bayesian unfolding of
the same inputs differs from the method on the sixteen five-observable cells. Fake-data studies
at a GiBUU-like truth and at a synthetic momentum-transfer tilt find residual bias exceeding
uncertainty-based criteria (p < 0.01, or any cell with |z| > 2) that were fixed before the tests
were graded. Rounding-level changes of the inputs move the results by up to 2.6 statistical
standard deviations, beyond a stability requirement fixed in advance. The coverage of the
reported uncertainty intervals has not been validated: the declared five-observable coverage test
did not run to a result, and a declared test of the procedure’s two-dimensional covariance at
two held-out tunes reads under-covers. The sixteen-cell output on data, with a median relative
uncertainty per cell of 7.86%, including a run-period contribution estimated after differences
between periods were observed, and descriptive comparisons with generator predictions, is given
only as an illustration.

Executive summary
We apply unbinned machine-learning unfolding (OmniFold [1, 2]) to MINERvA’s medium-energy
neutrino data. This note is the technical record of a methods and benchmark study of that unfolding.
Applied to the data, the five-observable estimator gives a cross section on the sixteen cells of §10
in pT , p∥ , Eavail , q3 and W , as illustrative output, not for fitting or for constraining models. Its
covariance gives a median per-cell uncertainty of 7.86%. As a descriptive comparison, three external

generator predictions, compared on all sixteen cells, give χ2 between 107 and 229 for 16 degrees
of freedom. GiBUU, compared only on the 8 cells its sample covers, gives χ2 = 48 on those cells
(§10.7).
The unbinned result for d2 σ/(dpT dp∥ ) reproduces the published integrated cross section [3]
within about one percent, with a correlated shape difference (§6). Whether it is ready for publication
as a measurement has not been established. LightGBM was chosen over the exact-split estimator,
which is kept as a cross-check, after both comparisons with the publication were known (§3.2). The
background-subtraction treatment of the 2D result was decided on a closure test before the rebuilt
result’s distance from the published result was computed, and the rebuilt result was adopted after
that distance was known (§3.2); the comparison with the publication is therefore not an independent
test of the method.
Adding Eavail , q3 and W gives simultaneous unfolds in three, four and five dimensions; the 3D,
4D and configuration-(a) five-dimensional unfolds are reported as central values. In configuration (a),
the purity-subtracted estimator of the five-dimensional central value, the 4D and 5D marginalnormalization checks against the lower-dimensional result pass to ≈1–2 %. The 3D marginal of
configuration (e), a deterministic estimator with refined negative-weight subtraction, agrees to
0.141 % in total with the 2D result of configuration (f), the nominal estimator with that subtraction,
a cross-configuration comparison (§7). Reconstructed W is fixed by (pT , p∥ , q3 ), so the five are
simultaneous, not independent, observables (§8.2).
The generator comparisons of the central values are descriptive. All four truth-level generators,
GENIE [4], MINERvA Tune [5], NuWro [6] and GiBUU [7], under-predict the unfolded central
value in the quasielastic–∆ dip (0.1 ≤ Eavail < 0.4 GeV), the region of the established MINERvA
low-recoil discrepancy [5, 8] (§7.6, §7.7). In cell-integrated terms, most of the positive difference
from the Tune v1 prior lies at Eavail ≥ 0.8 GeV, much of it at W ≥ 1.8 GeV (§8.4). The high-Eavail ,
high-W region carries the largest regularization bias measured in §9. The four external generators
also fall below the data in this region, each by about its overall shortfall once GiBUU’s sample limit
is allowed for (§8.5).
The generator tests are an application that illustrates calibrated inference with a biased estimator.
Calibrated joint five-dimensional tests of hybrid null hypotheses for five predictions, in total (rate
and shape) and in shape, reject all ten under six stated conditions (§11.4). The rejections are not
physical exclusions, and matched coarse projections reject as well (§11.7). The pseudo-experiments
lost to time limits have been rerun, for reporting only, so every submitted draw has been observed.
No recovered draw changes a decision (§11.6). Correcting the carbon-only NuWro and GiBUU
predictions to the CH target moves both toward the data on the joint cells; on the sixteen cells
GiBUU’s total χ2 rises (§11.9). The ten decisions, made with the carbon-only predictions, are
unchanged. In the descriptive re-evaluation with corrected predictions, NuWro’s total-test rejection
is not robust to the correction (§11.9).
A methodological finding concerns the standard covariance, which sums single-source systematic
matrices, each with all other sources nominal, and so omits nonlinear cross-source response terms.
A corrected unified-throw test, which re-unfolds with all reweightable sources perturbed together,
stores the joint mean shift separately (§4.5).
A preliminary extended-fiducial study removes the truth-muon cuts and keeps the fiducial volume
and MINOS-based selection. It puts about 6 % of the scalar 2D rate in newly acceptance-supported
cells (part of tier 1, the interpreted measurement) and about 28 % in low- or zero-efficiency cells
(tier 2, extrapolated from the prior; §12).
The body states each limitation with the result it qualifies; the principal ones are these. Ck
denotes coverage within ±k standard deviations. The quoted 2D statistical block has had no coverage
test; the fixed-truth coverage studies scored two earlier statistical bands, of which the first-built

undercovers in its tails (C2 = 0.912, vs. 0.954; §4.2, §5.2). At two held-out GENIE v3 tunes, a
declared test of the analysis procedure’s 2D covariance, remade at each tune’s truth, reads undercovers (§5.3). No result’s intervals have validated coverage (two dimensions: §4.6; five dimensions:
§9.5; the sixteen cells: §10.5). The others are stated with their results: the generator comparisons
(§§7–8); the absence of a 3D or 4D covariance (§7); the five-dimensional estimator’s regularization
bias (§9.3) and the purity-subtraction bias of configurations (a) and (c) (§9.2); five-dimensional
intervals and their coverage (§9.4, §9.5); the five-axis covariance of the configuration-(a) central
value (§9.6); the calibration and nuisance model of the generator tests (§11.3, §11.8); tier 2 of
the extended-fiducial study (§12); the fake-data limitation of the sixteen-cell result (§10.5); the
dependence of the sixteen cells on the unfolding algorithm (§10.6); and point-cloud work (App. F).

6

Introduction and motivation

MINERvA (Main Injector Experiment for ν-A) is a dedicated neutrino–nucleus scattering experiment
in Fermilab’s NuMI beamline, immediately upstream of the MINOS near detector [9]. During
roughly a decade of operation ending in 2019, it recorded high-statistics samples in low-energy and
medium-energy (ME) beam configurations [10] and in neutrino-dominated (forward horn current,
FHC) and antineutrino-dominated modes. MINERvA measures neutrino–nucleus cross sections
on a range of nuclear targets in the few-GeV region most relevant to accelerator-based oscillation
experiments. These cross sections and their nuclear effects are a leading systematic uncertainty
for T2K [11], NOvA [12], Hyper-Kamiokande [13], and DUNE [14]. The few-GeV regime combines
quasielastic, resonant, and deep-inelastic processes that no single model describes well. This analysis
uses the public ME FHC CC-inclusive data product (detector details in §2).
The MINERvA ME FHC charged-current (CC) inclusive double-differential cross section in muon
transverse and longitudinal momentum, d2 σ/(dpT dp∥ ), was published by Ruterbories et al. [3] using
D’Agostini iterative Bayesian unfolding (IBU) [15] with a binned response matrix. We reproduce it
with unbinned OmniFold [1, 2], which iteratively reweights events using machine-learned likelihood
ratios on per-event observables rather than on a fixed histogram binning.
Machine-learning advances have enabled unbinned unfolding (overviews in Refs. [16, 17]; crossexperiment guidance in Ref. [18]). One broad family iteratively reweights a starting simulation;
OmniFold [1, 2, 19] is its most widely adopted method. The other generates new samples directly
with a neural network [20–23]. Both lift the binned-response limitations of widely-used classical
methods [24] such as IBU, the SVD method [25], and TUnfold [26].
Prior OmniFold applications. In the last few years OmniFold has progressed from a method
proposal to published measurements. H1 published the first real-data applications in deep-inelastic
scattering: lepton–jet correlations unfolded simultaneously in eight dimensions [27], followed by jet
substructure at high Q2 [28]. LHCb unfolded identified-hadron distributions in Z-tagged jets [29].
ATLAS performed a simultaneous unbinned measurement of twenty-four Z+jets observables [30].
Ref. [18] collects these and other applications and distills cross-experiment practice. Three of the
practices it audits appear in our uncertainty budget: the ensemble-mean cross-check of the central
value, the bootstrap statistical covariance, and the joint-perturbation (unified-throw) covariance
check of §4.5.
H1 cut backgrounds to a negligible level; LHCb corrected per track before unfolding. ATLAS left
its less-than-0.25 % background in place and assigned it as an uncertainty, while identifying negativelyweighted simulated events as the natural in-method subtraction [2, 30]. Ref. [18] recommends this
in-method subtraction for nontrivial backgrounds. Recent methodological work explicitly treats
backgrounds, fakes, acceptance, and inefficiency in OmniFold-HI [31] and generative unfolding [32].
These constructions address related problems; their subtraction prescriptions can differ.
Closest to this analysis is the study of Huang et al. [33], the first application of OmniFold to a
neutrino cross section: a mock-data unfolding of muon kinematics on the public T2K near-detector
simulated dataset, benchmarked against traditional binned approaches. Two of its findings bear on
neutrino applications. The first is its iteration count, which we do not adopt (§3.2). The second
is an ensemble of reweighting trials with different train/test splits that controls the ML stochastic
component; we include that component explicitly, as the training-seed variation band (§4). The
present work differs from that study in three ways. It unfolds real MINERvA data to an absolutely
normalized cross section, with a 2D uncertainty budget whose covariance components are all built
with the central value’s own learner (§3.2). Its gradient-boosted decision-tree (GBDT) learners
suit a low-dimensional tabular feature set. It compares the result directly with a published binned

measurement of the same data (§6); because two analysis choices were adopted after that comparison
was known (§3.2), the comparison is descriptive, not a blind validation.
Motivation for unbinned unfolding. First, OmniFold learns event weights in the full observable
space and histograms only the final result, so reported bins can be chosen or refined after unfolding
without rerunning it. Background subtraction is unbinned too in the 2D result and its covariance,
in the 3D and 4D central values and in the sixteen-cell five-dimensional result (§10), which use
refined negative-weight subtraction (§3.1). The configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value and
its five-axis covariance, and the extended-fiducial value, use the binned purity weight, a per-reco-bin
(D − B)/D weight fixed before training. Second, additional observables enter as input features,
without multiplying response-matrix bins. The available-energy extension (§7) is built this way; a
binned method such as IBU would need a correspondingly larger multidimensional response matrix
to include it. Third, OmniFold regularizes through model capacity and through an iteration count,
which it shares with IBU. Comparing the two methods on the same data probes the result’s method
dependence (§6).
Analysis strategy. The published 2D cross section provides a quantitative, descriptive comparison
for unbinned OmniFold on MINERvA data and a size comparison for its uncertainty budget (§§2–6).
The same pipeline then adds Eavail , q3 (§7) and W (§8) as classifier inputs, tested by marginalization
to each lower-dimensional result, closure, coverage and classifier-family comparisons. The validation
and uncertainty of the five-dimensional estimator (§9), the sixteen-cell result (§10) and the calibrated
generator tests built on the estimator (§11) follow these central values. A scalar study with relaxed
truth-muon cuts (§12) probes an alternative signal definition. The companion journal article is a
shortened account, and this note gives the detail it omits.
Scope. The results hold for one implementation of unbinned OmniFold, applied to one data set,
and for the configurations, observables and deformations tested. The principal limitations are listed
in the executive summary (p. 2). The generator tests of §11 evaluate hybrid null hypotheses and add
no reportable uncertainty. Full-event unfolding with point-cloud transformer (PET) representations
is in progress and documented separately (App. F), and no result in this note depends on it.

2

The MINERvA experiment, data and simulation samples

2.1

Detector and beam

MINERvA [9] was a fine-grained scintillator-tracker neutrino experiment on Fermilab’s NuMI
beamline. Its central tracker comprises hexagonal planes of triangular polystyrene scintillator
strips in three stereo orientations, surrounded by electromagnetic and hadronic calorimetry. The
magnetized MINOS near detector, 2 m downstream, serves as the muon spectrometer, measuring
momentum and charge for MINOS-matched tracks. We use the medium-energy (ME) neutrino-mode
(FHC) exposure with ⟨Eν ⟩ ∼ 6 GeV. The flux is the a posteriori constrained NuMI ME prediction
of Ref. [10], combining PPFX hadron-production weights and the neutrino–electron-scattering
constraint, identical to the published measurement’s flux [3].

2.2

Data and simulation samples

The twelve ME FHC playlists (ME1A–ME1G, ME1L–ME1P) contain 1.057 × 1021 protons on target,
the same exposure as Ref. [3]. The selection below leaves 4 119 797 reconstructed data events.

The reference simulation uses GENIE 2.12.6 [4] and MINERvA Tune v1, applied as per-event
weights by the reweighting tools of the MINERvA Analysis Toolkit (MAT). These are the flux-andcentral-value (flux-and-CV) weight, the GENIE model-parameter (“dial”) weights, the low-recoil
2p2h enhancement fit, and the non-resonant-pion reduction. The full Geant4-based [34] MINERvA
detector simulation underlies the simulated pre-selected analysis ntuples (AnaTuples). The simulated
sample contains 32 849 103 true signal events, POT-normalized event-by-event to the data exposure
and processed with identical reconstruction and selection.

2.3

Signal definition and event selection

The signal definition and phase space follow Ref. [3] exactly. Truth-level signal events are chargedcurrent νµ interactions in the central-tracker fiducial volume with muon phase space
θµ < 20◦ ,

1.5 < p∥ < 60 GeV/c,

pT < 4.5 GeV/c.

The fiducial volume spans 5980 < z < 8422 mm within an 850 mm apothem and contains 3.2353×1030
nucleons, the fixed tracker-geometry constant.
Reconstructed events require a vertex in the same fiducial volume and apothem, a muon track
with θµ < 20◦ matched to a track in the MINOS near detector whose MINOS track fit succeeded, no
dead-time overlap, and a neutrino (rather than antineutrino) candidate.
Genuine backgrounds are reconstructed events whose true interaction fails the signal definition:
charged-current events of other flavors or wrong sign, neutral-current interactions, and out-of-fiducialvertex events. Their predicted rate is 0.35 % of selected events, evaluated on playlist 1A. This
is the same sub-percent scale as the 8655 (0.2 %) predicted background events of Ref. [3], which
counts only the wrong-flavor, wrong-sign and neutral-current components; exact agreement is not
expected given the differences in definition and selection, and neither prediction carries an uncertainty.
Out-of-phase-space signal, reconstructed inside the phase space with its true muon outside it, is
treated separately. Both are POT-scaled and subtracted from the reconstructed-data target before
unfolding (§3).
Event selection determines which observations enter the analysis and which truth population is
being estimated. Classifier features determine what the learner can use within that population. A
muon can therefore be required for selection even when its momentum is absent from the classifier.

2.4

Per-event analysis trees

Unbinned unfolding needs per-event records rather than histograms. A dedicated event loop (App. H),
built on MAT’s systematic-universe tools, writes four per-event samples for each playlist: all true
signal events (the efficiency denominator), reconstructed truth-matched simulated events, simulated
background, and data. Entries carry truth and reconstructed observables (pT , p∥ , and in extended
runs Eavail , q3 , W ) and the MINERvA Tune v1 central-value weight. The systematic-universe samples
also carry every universe’s weight and the shifted observables of each lateral band, a variation that
shifts reconstructed kinematics rather than reweighting events (a vertical band; §4). Two conditions
make the truth sample the complete reference for the simulated signal:
• A reconstructed signal event is filled only if its (run, subrun, event) key is in the truth denominator. Truth events without a reconstructed partner enter as native OmniFold “miss”
entries.
• The simulated signal sample is complete by construction at ME FHC (completeness c =
1.000000; §3.4).
The loop reads one playlist-wide flux and calibration from the first event, so it is run separately
for each playlist, and the twelve per-playlist outputs are combined. The corresponding baseline
histogram event loop supplies the flux histogram, POT-weighted across playlists; playlist-to-playlist
flux differences are a ∼ 0.2 % effect.

2.5

Reco-level control distributions

Selected data exceed the POT-scaled MINERvA Tune v1 prediction (signal + background) by
∼ 12 % in overall normalization. Figure 1 compares all five reconstructed observables. The data/MC
ratio rises with pT , from 0.88 in the lowest bin to ∼ 1.2 above 1 GeV/c. Excess and trend together
reflect the truth-level normalization deficit of §6.5 and §7.6. The simulation describes the shapes
well, and the unfolding relies on the shapes; its reweighting toward the data corrects the rate offset.

The beam and acceptance set the shapes. The p∥ peak at 4–6 GeV/c reflects the ⟨Eν ⟩ ≈ 6 GeV
NuMI medium-energy peak; spectrometer matching removes the lowest-p∥ muons. The pT spectrum
peaks near 0.7 GeV/c and falls by over an order of magnitude across the measured range. Low-recoil
(quasi-elastic and 2p2h) events dominate the sample. Available energy Eavail concentrates below
∼0.5 GeV, with a long inelastic tail in the wide upper bins; q3 tracks this population’s momentum
transfer. Reconstructed W rises through the smeared quasi-elastic and ∆(1232) region before the
deep-inelastic continuum takes over above ∼2 GeV. Absolute background is at the few-percent level
under the muon projections and concentrates at high recoil (highest Eavail , q3 and W bins). Beyond
the pT trend noted above, the ratio panels also show a mild enhancement in the lowest Eavail bins,
reflecting the low-recoil excess examined at truth level in §7.7.

Figure 2: Reco-level corner plot of five observables. The lower triangle shows pairwise POT-scaled
MC (signal+background) densities on a logarithmic color scale, with wide catch bins dropped. Each
panel annotates the simulation’s weighted Pearson correlation and the corresponding data value.
The diagonal shows 1D marginals (data points over MC). The strongest correlations follow from

muon variables. Data reproduce every simulated coefficient within 0.02.

3

The unbinned OmniFold procedure

3.1

Algorithm

OmniFold [1, 2] unfolds by iterated reweighting with machine-learned likelihood ratios, operating on
per-event observables rather than on a fixed histogram binning. Each iteration has two steps:
1. Step 1 (detector space). A classifier separates measured data from reconstructed simulation
weighted by the current push-weights. Its calibrated output p gives the likelihood-ratio reweight

w = p/(1 − p) for each reconstructed simulated event. Each simulated event is a (truth, reco)
pair, allowing the weight to be pulled back directly to its truth record. Truth events without
a reconstructed partner (“misses”) receive step-1 weights from a regressor trained on the
reconstructed subsample, w(truth features). This native OmniFold acceptance treatment
requires no separate efficiency correction.
2. Step 2 (truth space). A second classifier separates the original truth-level simulation from
the same sample carrying the pulled-back step-1 weights. Its smooth truth-space reweight
becomes the next iteration’s push-weight.
After n iterations, the push-weights define an efficiency-corrected truth-level reweighted simulation
ensemble. Histogramming this ensemble in any binning chosen afterwards gives the unfolded spectrum.
The iteration count and learner capacity regularize the unfolding.
Training details. Each classifier is trained on the full sample whose weights it then sets, with no
held-out or cross-fitted split and no early stopping. The two classes keep their physical weights, with
no class balancing and no renormalization between iterations, so the learned ratio p/(1 − p), with p
restricted to [10−6 , 1 − 10−6 ], carries the normalization. Truth events without a reconstructed partner
receive the prediction of an unweighted regressor of the step-1 weight on the truth observables. In
the refined configurations, a classifier separating positive from negative weights, trained with |w|,
gives each event the weight |w| max(0, 2g − 1). No effective sample size of the unfolded ensemble is
computed.
Background subtraction. In the binned purity subtraction, all selected data events enter step 1;
none is discarded and no negative-weight events are injected. Each receives a reco-bin purity weight.
The POT-scaled background prediction includes genuine backgrounds and out-of-phase-space signal
(§2.3) and is binned on the reconstructed analysis grid. A data event in reco bin b receives

wpur (b) = max 0, Ndata (b) − Nbkg (b) Ndata (b),
the predicted signal purity, floored at zero in the rare bins where the background prediction exceeds
the data. The step-1 classifier retains the full unbinned kinematics. Before the final histogramming,
binning enters, by design, only through this multiplicative correction, an ∼3 % effect overall, largest
where backgrounds concentrate (§2.3).
Other experiments’ treatments are summarized in §1. At the sub-percent genuine-background
scale, a per-reco-bin weight is a conservative middle ground. A fully unbinned alternative injects
negatively weighted background simulation on the measured side, targeting the identical backgroundsubtracted density (D − B)/S. It reproduces the binned correction to −0.13 % on the total 2D
cross section and its systematic and statistical covariances to within 2 % (App. C). It removes
residual binning dependence and suits the extended-fiducial analysis, where backgrounds are larger.
The quoted 2D result and its covariance (App. C, §6), the 3D and 4D central values (§7) and the
sixteen-cell 5D result and its covariance (§10) use its refined form; so does the refined arm of the
background-bias and generator-sized-change studies (§9.2), the held-out-shape and precision studies
(§§9.3–9.4) and the generator tests. Every other quoted cross section and uncertainty, including the
configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value and the extended-fiducial value, uses the binned
correction. Table 2 (§3.2) gives the configuration behind each result.

3.2

Estimators and configuration

Gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDTs) replace the original OmniFold neural networks for the
unfolding of scalar observables. With at most five high-level features per event, tree ensembles
typically match or outperform neural networks [35] and train deterministically and far faster on
CPU. Ref. [36] demonstrates their effectiveness for unbinned unfolding with few high-level features,
complementing deep models for low-level event representations. We use GBDTs for all unfolding
results in this note (point-cloud representations: App. F). Calibration tests and MLP/transformer
cross-checks test the implementation in §7.5.
The unfolding code supports interchangeable learners (component terminology and code paths in
App. H). The quoted 2D central value and all of its uncertainty ensembles use LightGBM with refined
negative-weight background subtraction (App. C), configuration (f) of Table 2. These ensembles are
the systematic-universe sweep, the Poisson bootstrap, the training-seed variations and the run-period
unfoldings. The step-1 classifier, the step-2 classifier and the regressor for unreconstructed truth
events share one setting: 100 trees, 8 leaves and learning rate 0.1. The central value is the universe
sweep’s own central value at seed 42, so the systematic covariance shares its estimator and seed
(§4.1). The quoted statistical covariance, from a bootstrap drawn in three independent streams
(data, signal and background simulation), also uses seed 42, and the training-seed covariance uses
five seed settings that include seed 42 (§4.2, §4.3).
A central unfolding at seed 45 differs from the seed-42 one by at most 1.4 % in a cell. With the
purity-based subtraction (configuration (a)), the seed-42 central value differs from the seed-1 one
by a per-bin median of 0.18 % (maximum 1.79 %), or 0.30 (maximum 1.28) of the per-bin standard
deviation of the earlier statistical band, built at seed 1. Two regenerations, with one driver and one
input and differing only in the seed, reproduce the two central values to ≲ 2 × 10−11 , so the difference
is attributable to the seed. The quoted central value records its run configuration; it repeats to
9 × 10−12 and equals the refined 2D data unfold of App. C.6 to 7 × 10−12 . The purity-subtracted
seed-42 central value has no recorded run configuration. An independent rerun reproduces it to
1.4 × 10−11 , and so does a regeneration whose run configuration is recorded (Ref. [37], R1.12).
Against the ten purity-subtracted LightGBM central values at seeds 1–10 it is mildly over-dispersed
(RMS standardized difference 1.28, maximum 4.25). Seed 42 was chosen on computing cost: building
the statistical band at seed 42 was the cheaper of the two costed ways to give the central value and
the statistical band a common seed.
The exact-split cross-check. The exact-split configuration (exact) uses scikit-learn GradientBoosting with single-threaded exact-split CART. Its seed was not fixed, and its run log was not
found; two driver revisions regenerate it to 7 × 10−15 . It is not invariant to the estimator seed,
however: one reseeded run (random_state 1/2/3) moves every bin, by a median of 0.18 % (maximum
1.6 %), and gives χ2 /ndf = 3.57. The spread over seeds is unmeasured. The reseeded run’s median
agrees with the 0.18 % LightGBM seed-1 to seed-42 median above, but the agreement is coincidental.
The exact-split central value differs from the purity-subtracted LightGBM seed-42 central value by a
per-bin median of 0.98 % (maximum 12.2 %), 1.32 statistical standard deviations. It differs from the
quoted central value, which also differs from it in background treatment, by a per-bin median of
1.03 % (maximum 17.6 %). It gives χ2 /ndf = 3.66 (unseeded, reproducible) against the published
covariance, against 2.65 for the purity-subtracted LightGBM central value and 2.27 for the quoted
one (§6.4). It has no matched covariance: no exact-split bootstrap, universe sweep or seed scan exists.
LightGBM was chosen for the central value after both comparisons with the publication were known,
so that the central value and its covariance share an estimator. A third learner, the scikit-learn
histogram backend (hist, HistGradientBoosting, matched 8-leaf configuration), is retained for

method-dependence studies (§6.4).
The estimator configurations. Six estimator configurations, labeled (a)–(f), are used; Table 2
gives each one’s learner, background treatment and results, and each result names the configuration
it uses. All use five OmniFold iterations and gradient-boosted trees of nominal capacity (100 trees, 8
leaves) in both OmniFold steps; “deterministic” means fixed seeds and reproducible training. Results
from different configurations are not combined. Four are described in this section:
• (f), the nominal LightGBM configuration above with refined negative-weight subtraction and
the refinement classifier of (e), seeded at the estimator seed plus three: the 2D central value
(seed 42) and its uncertainty ensembles (§6);
• (a), the same learner with purity-based background subtraction (§3.1): the five-dimensional
central value (§8), the 3D and 4D injected-bump closures and Figs. 17 and 23 (§7), and the
purity-subtracted 2D comparisons of this section and §6.4;
• (e), the joint-test estimator: a deterministic LightGBM variant with the unbinned negativeweight background treatment (App. C), whose refinement classifier has 400 trees and 31
leaves. The negative-weight treatment reduces the purity method’s nominal-truth bias in the
highest-W column at least fifteen-fold (from 3.5 % to 4.1 % to 0.11 % to 0.24 %, the level of the
no-background closure), and the larger refinement classifier removes most of the backgroundrelated bias that remains (§§9.2–9.3). It gives the 3D and 4D central values (§7) and the
sixteen-cell five-dimensional result (§10). It is used for the generator tests (§11), the study
of generator shapes held out of its development (§9.3) and the precision study (§9.4). It was
selected before the joint-test design was fixed (§11);
• (b), the exact-split cross-check above.
Configuration (c) is the deterministic variant with purity-based subtraction, and (d) is the same
variant with negative weights and a nominal-capacity refinement; they serve the background-bias,
generator-sized-change and coverage studies listed in the table. The five-dimensional central value of
§8 and its five-axis covariance use configuration (a): they were produced before the purity bias was
found and the refinement was developed. A refined five-dimensional data unfolding (configuration (e))
gives the sixteen-cell result; its reported covariance on the sixteen cells is described in §10.3. The 2D
central value and its covariance were rebuilt with configuration (f) after a two-dimensional closure
test with backgrounds found the purity bias (App. C.6). The change was decided on that closure
test, before the rebuilt result’s distance from the published result was computed, and the rebuilt
result was adopted after that distance was known. The exact-split central value was the quoted 2D
central value until 2026-10-11; studies labeled exact-split use it and have not been recomputed. The
3D and 4D central values have been regenerated with configuration (e); no 3D or 4D covariance is
quoted.
Hyperparameter choice. The learner settings were fixed a priori; no hyperparameter search was
performed on data, which avoids tuning on the result. They are the scikit-learn GradientBoosting
defaults: 100 trees, learning rate 0.1, and depth-3 capacity. The LightGBM learner of the central
value and its ensembles uses matched capacity (num_leaves = 8 = 23 ), giving every learner the
same deliberately modest capacity, one of OmniFold’s regularizers (§3.1). The configuration is
instead validated after the fact by closure and classifier-calibration tests (§5, §7.5), insensitivity to
doubling the iteration count, measured for the exact-split cross-check (below), backend agreement
in method-dependence studies (§6.4), and an explicit training-seed variation band for residual ML
stochasticity (§4).

Production uses n = 5 iterations. Huang et al. [33] select 45 iterations for OmniFold-family
methods (20 for their binned benchmark) using a binned χ2 against known mock-data truth, a
criterion they note is infeasible on real data. We do not adopt that recommendation. Their mock
data injects a large prior–data shape discrepancy through an RPA-modified quasielastic model,
requiring many iterations. The simulation prior already describes the measured shapes well (§2.5);
the correction the data impose on it is dominantly one of normalization. Cross-experiment production
also typically uses ∼5 iterations [18]. Our n = 5 is instead justified empirically: for the exact-split
cross-check, the total cross section agrees to within 0.026 % between five and ten iterations (§5), and
the unfolded result reproduces the integrated cross section of an independently published binned
measurement of the same data (§6).

3.3

MINERvA driver and pipeline conventions

The higher-dimensional MINERvA driver (App. H) extends the 2D driver using an axis list and the
same 2D helpers. The first two axes are always (pT , p∥ ), carrying the fiducial phase-space cut and
the per-pT flux. Higher-dimensional runs extend the muon observables (pT , p∥ ) with Eavail , q3 , W in
the phase space of §2.3. Every production, universe-sweep, and closure run follows these conventions:
• Reco-space masking. The measured events entering step-1 training are exactly those passing
the reconstructed-level selection; nothing outside the reconstructed phase space reaches the
classifier.
• Step-1 training within the phase space. Step 1 targets the background-subtracted data
of §3.1, purity-weighted or with background simulation injected at negative weight (refined to
non-negative weights in the refined configurations). Genuine backgrounds and out-of-phasespace signal are removed from the data side. The simulated reconstructed sample contains no
out-of-phase-space signal by construction, so neither side of the step-1 training contains events
whose truth lies outside the measurement phase space.
• At histogramming, each simulated truth event carries its final OmniFold push-weight times
its central-value simulation weight, including MINERvA Tune v1 reweighting. No efficiencyhistogram division is applied: the step-1 regression for unreconstructed truth events already
gives an efficiency-corrected truth spectrum, and dividing again would double-correct.
• Closure mirrors production. In closure tests the pseudo-data are simulated events passing
both the reconstructed and truth selections, weighted identically to the production data path,
so closure exercises the same code path as the measurement.

3.4

Cross-section extraction

The differential cross section follows from unfolded truth-level counts:
dn σ
U
=
× 104 ,
dx1 · · · dxn
c Φ N POT ∆x1 · · · ∆xn
where U is the unfolded yield and c the per-bin completeness, the fraction of truth events visible to
the unfolding input. The event loop appends the truth events without a reconstructed partner, so
every truth event enters and c ≡ 1 bin-by-bin. The factor remains a monitored self-check; dropping
those events would give c < 1. Φ is the per-pT flux integral, broadcast over other axes, N the fiducial
nucleon count (3.2353 × 1030 ), and POT the data exposure (1.057 × 1021 ). The factor 104 converts
flux tabulated per square meter to the reported cm2 (1 m2 = 104 cm2 ), giving units of cm2 per axis

unit per nucleon. The implementation (App. H) is self-tested to reproduce stored lower-dimensional
reference extractions to < 10−12 . Since σ ∝ 1/Φ, Φ varies per flux universe when building the
systematic covariance. This variation dominates the Flux band (§4).
For the higher-dimensional extractions the integrated flux is Φ = 6.243 × 10−8 cm−2 per proton on
target, from 0 to 100 GeV after the neutrino–electron-scattering constraint, and the target contains
3.235 × 1030 nucleons.

3.5

Binning

The 2D grid has 14 pT × 16 p∥ bins; 205 of 224 are reported. The 19 unreported diagonal cells
lo
◦
have phi
T /p∥ > tan 20 . The extension axes use Eavail : [0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4, 0.8, 1.5, 3.0, 100] GeV; q3 :
[0, 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8, 1.2, 2.0, 100] GeV; W : [0, 1.1, 1.4, 1.8, 2.2, 3.0, 100] GeV. Each ends in a wide catch
bin to capture the full CC-inclusive tails in marginals; truncating the top edge would break the
marginalization checks of §7.3. Binning after unfolding allows these choices to be revisited without
re-running the unfolding. App. G (§G.3) gives all five axes’ edges. §A.3.1 defines the 205 reported
cells; the authoritative mapping is the bin-mapping file of the published data release [3].
Neither underflow nor overflow appears in the reported product, by construction rather than by
truncation: the unbinned unfolding has no response-matrix under- or overflow, and the reporting
bins are applied once, to events that all lie inside the grids. Truth-level muon edges coincide with the
measurement phase space (§2.3); no signal is defined beyond them. Hadronic axes span [0, 100] GeV,
with catch-bin edges far beyond the kinematic reach at ⟨Eν ⟩ ∼ 6 GeV, so the truth grids are
exhaustive. A selected reco event outside the grid is treated as unaccepted and excluded from the
measured sample by §3.3’s masking convention. Its in-phase-space truth content is recovered, as for
any unreconstructed event, by the treatment of truth events without a reconstructed partner.
Truth→reco migration on the analysis binning (Fig. 3) summarizes the smearing OmniFold must
undo, although it uses no response matrix. Median diagonal purity is ∼ 0.6 on every axis. At
migrations of this size a bin-by-bin correction would be badly biased, so unfolding is required.
The structure differs qualitatively between the muon and hadron sides. The spectrometer muon
measurement makes the pT and p∥ maps nearly tridiagonal, with migrations rarely exceeding one
bin. Purity dips to ∼0.45–0.5 for pT and ∼0.35 for p∥ where bins are finest, recovering in wide edge
bins. The calorimetric axes have coarser bins but relatively broader, visibly asymmetric migrations.
Eavail preferentially migrates downward because energy carried by neutrons and other unresponsive
particles is not collected. Diagonal purity falls from 0.61 in the lowest bins to 0.43 in the highest. q3 ,
built from Eavail and the muon, inherits a milder version of the same pattern. W compounds Eavail
and muon smearing and has the broadest migrations; two-bin shifts are common in the resonance
region. The lowest-W (quasi-elastic) bin remains purest at 0.71.

3.6

Related developments and estimator scope

Profile OmniFold [38] extends classifier-based EM to forward-model nuisance parameters. Its distinct
algorithmic construction complements earlier unbinned profiled unfolding [19]. Profiling nuisances
with observed data would change our inference model, which propagates an external nuisance
ensemble through a fixed procedure. Neither a profiled estimator nor its uncertainty is used here.
SPINUP learns a forward mapping [39]; AUSSIE replaces OmniFold’s second step with a different
loss and avoids the outer iteration [40]. Both offer possible future cross-checks with different
algorithmic assumptions. Reduced simulation-prior dependence does not establish detector-model
independence, adequate phase-space support, or coverage for this measurement. Comparisons would
require matched signal, selection, normalization, and uncertainty definitions.

4

Uncertainty quantification: constructing the covariance blocks

The uncertainty budget sums four blocks: systematic universes, statistical bootstraps, ML trainingseed variation, and a run-period block (§4.5). Their independence is an assumption: distinct random
streams and physics sources do not by themselves establish it after unfolding. The universe, statistical
and training-seed blocks are computed with the LightGBM estimator of the quoted 2D central value
(§3.2); the universe and statistical blocks use its training seed (seed 42), and the training-seed block
uses five seed settings that include it. A 3D budget built with the analogous 3D grid construction is
not quoted, and no 4D uncertainty magnitude is reported (§7.8). App. A gives the full mathematics
and worked examples using the reported numbers (N-D declarations in App. B). The resulting
budget and its agreement with the published total are in §6.3; what the covariance includes and
omits, and the conventions it follows, are in §4.6.

4.1

Systematic universes

Detector and model systematics use the July universe sweep: the 44 error bands and 187 universes
of the MINERvA Analysis Toolkit (MAT), identical to the standard systematics set of the published
analysis. Each universe is re-unfolded with LightGBM at seed 42 and compared with the quoted
2D central value, unfolded with the same estimator and seed. The five lateral bands, which
shift reconstructed kinematics rather than event weights, are taken from re-unfolds of re-selected
universes (§4.7) at the same seed. The band sum omits the nonresonant-pion band Rvp1pi, which
encodes the same shift as Rvn1pi (§4.6); it holds 43 bands plus the flat normalization. Per-band
covariances follow MAT’s universe-mean centering and biased 1/N normalization. For a ±1σ pair
the covariance is the outer product of the half-difference of the two shifts, excluding any common
endpoint displacement from the central value (CV). The 100 PPFX hadron-production flux universes
use the same mean-centered 1/N formula.
Ranked by per-bin median fractional standard deviation, the largest bands of the 2D universe block
are the flux (3.71 %, with MAT’s ν–e and inverse-muon-decay constraint weights), the MINOS muon
energy scale (Muon_Energy_MINOS, 2.36 %), the MINOS muon-match efficiency (MinosEfficiency,
1.50 %) and the MINERvA muon energy scale (Muon_Energy_MINERvA, 1.44 %). Figure 4 shows
the fractional uncertainty grouped as in Figs. 6–7 of the published measurement; the full 205 × 205
covariance is projected exactly, C1D = P C2D P ⊤ , before division by the 1D central value.

Flux 1/Φ normalization. Varying Φ per flux universe in the dominant σ ∝ 1/Φ term gives a
Flux band of 3.71 % with the constraint weights, and a combined budget, from our covariance alone,
of 6.41 %. Target-nucleon normalization contributes a fully-correlated rank-1 band of 1.4 %. The
two MINOS muon-energy-scale universes carry the correlated-flux weight, so each is divided by its
own flux, not by the CV flux; that band’s integral σ is 0.30 %. The unweighted 100-universe flux
covariance has participation-ratio effective rank ≈ 1. Its leading mode correlates at ρ = 0.900 with
the rank-1 muon-energy-scale band of the joint flux and muon-energy-scale block of Ref. [41], as
rederived here. The cross term is rank-2; two independent estimators give ρ = 0.87–0.90. Adding
this block leaves the systematic rank unchanged because its directions already lie in the universecovariance span (App. A.4). The quoted budget does not include this cross block: with the MINOS
universes divided by their own flux, the correlated-flux effect is already in the MINOS band, and

adding the block would count it twice.

4.2

Statistical uncertainty: Poisson bootstrap

The statistical block jointly bootstraps data and simulation with 300 per-event Poisson(1) weight
replicas, each fully re-unfolded with the LightGBM estimator at the central value’s training seed
(seed 42), and is their sample covariance about the replica mean (Fig. 5). Each replica draws its
weights in three independent streams, for data, signal simulation and background simulation; possible
shared events between the two simulations were not assessed. Because the background simulation
is resampled in every replica, the separable background-only term is contained in the statistical
block. The reconstructed-level and truth-level weights of a simulated event share one draw, which
preserves within-event migration correlations. Fixing the GBDT seed per p
replica separates bootstrap
and training-seed variances. With 300 replicas the Gaussian formula 1/ 2(N − 1) gives a relative
standard error of about 4.1 % on each per-bin σ, and the per-bin σ from a nested half (150) of the
replicas is a median 1.000 times that from all 300. The replica
√ mean differs from the central value by
a per-bin median of 4.37 standard errors of the mean (σ/ 300), or 0.25 of the statistical standard
deviation. Because the block is the covariance about the replica mean, it does not include this offset.
The bootstrap block is smaller than the published statistical block (ratio of square-root traces 0.407;
App. A.4).
The statistical block quoted here has had no coverage test: the fixed-truth coverage studies of
§5.2 scored two earlier statistical bands, not this block. No coverage test on held-out simulation
has been run, and no design for one has been registered in advance. A declared test of the analysis
procedure’s 2D covariance, remade at each of two held-out GENIE v3 tunes, reads under-covers
(§5.3); a held-out tune moves the truth, not the sample, and that test concerns the procedure’s
covariance at those tunes, not this block at the data. Because the fixed-truth test’s truth equals
the prior, it also cannot decide whether the smaller statistical block reflects an efficiency gain,
regularization bias or undercoverage.

4.3

ML/optimization uncertainty: training-seed variation

The training-seed block is the sample covariance, over five training-seed settings, of the mean of
20 bootstrap replicas at each setting. Each setting is a triplet (s, s+1, s+2) of seeds for the step-1
classifier, step-2 classifier and miss regressor, with s ∈ {42, 45, 48, 51, 54}; the block has rank 4
(§A.2.1). It is the smallest block, with a per-bin median fractional standard deviation of 0.048 %.
With four degrees of freedom, its variance has a Gaussian relative standard error of about 71 %.
Its per-bin σ is a median 0.27 of that of the earlier ten-seed block, because the bootstrap × seed
interaction, which dominated the earlier scan, is already inside the fixed-seed statistical block; the
training-seed block carries it only at its 1/20 share, a median 0.763 of its diagonal. The interaction
itself and the universe × retraining term are reported and not added (§4.6).
No block contains the dependence on the learner. The central value and the exact-split cross-check
differ by a per-bin median of 1.03 % (maximum 17.6 %; §3.2); they differ in background treatment as
well as in learner. One reseeded exact-split run differs from the unseeded one by a per-bin median of
0.18 % (maximum 1.6 %). The spread of the exact-split estimator over seeds is unmeasured (§3.2).

4.4

Further checks of the 2D covariance

Seven universes of the July sweep (§4.1) are reproduced to ≤ 1.4 × 10−11 by the committed driver;
the others are accepted by inference, because the same code produced them (Ref. [37], R1.16).
For two bands whose universes barely change the unfolding inputs, EtaNCEL and NormNCRES,
the responses are consistent with instability of the GBDT training contributing at the scale of the
training-seed variation (§4.3). They neither establish the mechanism for every small band nor justify
subtracting an overlap from the budget.
Each lateral band of the quoted universe block uses a single unfold at the central training seed,
so it includes that seed’s training noise. The flux-universe index identity behind the 1/Φu rescale is
established by the code chain and by an exact test: 900 events × 100 universes match to 5.5 × 10−14 ,
and a control shifted by one universe fails at ≥ 0.245 (Ref. [37], R1.13). In all 12 playlists the
production flux weights equal those of the constraint file and of the earlier 2D budget, with which
§4.6 compares the quoted construction.

4.5

Combination and the unified-throw test

The nominal covariance is the block sum C = Csyst + Cstat + CML + CPL , which assumes the blocks
are independent and the unfolding responds linearly to simultaneous perturbations, so that per-band
covariances add. The unified throw tests linearity directly (App. A.2.4; §9.6) by perturbing all
reweightable bands and the flux universes simultaneously in each event and re-unfolding jointly. The
procedure interpolates asymmetrically between actual −1σ and +1σ event-weight endpoints, fixes
the estimator seed for every throw, and centers the joint covariance on the throw mean. That mean’s
displacement from CV is stored separately as a per-bin vector and its norm; per-band endpoint
displacements are not stored. The matched block-sum comparator re-unfolds both endpoints of
each band using MAT’s universe-mean centering and biased 1/N normalization. A single-throw
superposition probe is insufficient to test nonlinear response. No scalar run-to-run jitter floor is
subtracted. The configuration-(a) 5D result uses the strict joint ensemble and matched-endpoint
comparator.
In the five-axis covariance of the configuration-(a) central value, CZ , the per-source decomposition
is retained for attribution and the joint throws enter through the inflation of Eq. (5) (§9.6).
The run-period block CPL is an uncertainty assigned for an observed inconsistency between run
periods whose cause is unknown; it is not a measured systematic uncertainty from an identified

source. Data taken in different run periods are not mutually consistent within the other uncertainties.
After unfolding, the double ratio (data over simulated truth) of the data-taking periods (playlists)
1G–1M to the earliest periods, 1A–1F, has a p∥ step of (+6.89 ± 0.46)% at 8 GeV/c. Diluted into
the combined result, the raw shift of those playlists is small in each bin: every marginal z is below
0.7. Its p∥ χ2 against the earlier 2D budget is nevertheless 34.4, where noise would give 2.0. These
data cannot establish which period, if any, is correct.
The block is built from separate seed-42 unfolds of three run periods, the playlist groups 1A–1F,
1G–1M and 1N–1P, each unfolded through its own simulated detector response. The block uses each
period’s unfolded result over that period’s own simulated truth. Each period is compared with the
exposure-weighted mean of the three, so no reference period is needed; the spread, applied to the
central value, gives the block. It is added after the other blocks. It has rank two, and with three
period groups its own size is poorly determined. Its per-bin median fractional standard deviation
is 2.09 % (84th percentile 3.34 %, maximum 6.48 %); it is 1.17 % to 3.36 % on the p∥ projections
and 1.97 % on the integrated cross section. Added to the other blocks, it raises the per-bin σ by
a median of 5.8 % (Table 3). Taking 1A–1F as the reference would give a smaller block (per-bin
median 1.38 %, integral 1.09 %).

The block’s interpretation is limited by the small number of period groups, by possible shrinkage
from the regularization, and by the statistical and training noise it retains. Because the block is
built from the observed differences among the periods, it contains them by construction; this carries
no information about its coverage, that is, about whether its size is right. For the same reason, the
double-ratio χ2 against any total that contains the block is bounded whatever the data, so it does
not test the block. The raw 1G–1M p∥ χ2 is 34.8 against the other blocks alone and 2.59 with the
block added; these values indicate sensitivity only.

4.6

Scope, conventions and limitations of the 2D covariance

Status of the 2D result. We report a reproducible two-dimensional central value, with a
covariance computed with the central value’s own estimator, under the conventions stated below.
The result’s intervals, bias and covariance have not been independently validated. In particular, the
coverage of its intervals has not been validated on independent simulated samples, nor simultaneously
over all reported bins and projections. At two held-out GENIE v3 tunes, a declared test of the
procedure’s 2D covariance remade at each tune’s truth reads under-covers, with a joint in-region count
of 0 of 300 at both (§5.3); it concerns the procedure’s covariance at those tunes, not the reported
intervals at the data. The covariance also gives no calibrated significance for comparisons with
generator predictions. Such validation would need an independent simulation sample of production
size, a validated generator of pseudo-data with continuously varied nuisance parameters, and samples
from independent event generators beyond the truth-level ones already examined; none is available.
We therefore make no claim about coverage, and goodness-of-fit values computed with this covariance
are indicative only. The following are not included in the covariance:

• the approximation in how the systematic-universe unfolds weight events that fail reconstruction,
whose effect has not been measured beyond the low-sensitivity test below;
• the dependence on the machine-learning estimator (§4.3);
• the dependence on the regularization, and departures of the detector response from its
parametrized family, which we assume absent; we assign no uncertainty for either;
• the dependence on the generator shape of the prior, which is reported beside the covariance as
Cmodel (below).
Generator-shape model dependence. The dependence of the result on the generator
shape
P
of the prior is reported beside the covariance, as a separate covariance Cmodel = k sk sT
k . For
each of three generators k (GENIE with meson-exchange currents, NuWro and GiBUU), the prior’s
truth distribution is reweighted by the ratio of that generator’s (pT , p∥ ) shape to GENIE’s, with
its normalization kept, and the data are unfolded again with the central value’s estimator; sk is
the per-cell relative change of the result. Each generator enters as one fully correlated component,
unscaled, so Cmodel has rank three. Its median fractional standard deviation over the 205 reported
cells is 3.71 % (84th percentile 7.10 %, maximum 21.10 %). For the sum of the combined covariance
and Cmodel , the total for users who include this dependence, the per-cell median is 7.90 % (84th
percentile 12.32 %, maximum 24.49 %). On the integrated cross section Cmodel gives 0.03 %, and
the total stays at 4.76 %: the reweightings change only the prior’s shape, so Cmodel does not test
the model dependence of the normalization. The comparison with the published uncertainty (§6.3)
uses the combined covariance alone, because the published uncertainty has no component for this
dependence either.
Cmodel measures generator-shape model dependence; it is not a calibrated bound on the bias in
each cell. A closure test with NuWro-shaped pseudo-data and the configuration-(a) estimator has a
bias above 1 % in 127 cells. In 67 of them the NuWro shift of the quoted result reaches the bias, and
in 72 the quadrature sum of the other two generators’ shifts reaches it; these counts are descriptive.
Where a pooled cell, a noise-gated cell, or an unreported cell meets another cell, the construction
can draw a step of more than 0.1 in a generator’s reweighting ratio; the cells beside such steps enter
with their measured shifts. Identified after the measurement, these 52 cells hold 59.4 % of the trace
of Cmodel and carry most of its large shifts.
Conventions. Each systematic band’s covariance is computed about the mean of its universes
(constraint-weighted for the flux), with 1/N normalization, not about the central value. This follows
the MAT treatment of universes (§4.1), which we use as a stated convention. Across bands, the
per-bin median offset of a band’s center from the central value is 0.10 % to 0.55 % of the central
value, or 0.09–2.85 times the band’s σ. The statistical block is the covariance about the replica mean
(§4.2).
Approximations and omitted terms. The systematic-universe unfolds approximate the weighting of events that fail reconstruction, which enters through each universe’s completeness; the
approximation is not corrected. It was tested in two of the 187 universes, MaRES_0 and the flux
universe with the largest median completeness change (Flux:23), each at seeds 42 and 43. In neither
was an effect resolved above the training-seed noise. The tests compared the difference with the σ of
the earlier ten-seed block, which is about 3.7 times that of the training-seed block used here. They
exclude only a median effect above twice that σ, so an effect below the training noise is not excluded.
Separately, retraining moves these two universes’ shifts by a median 1.13–1.23 times that σ.
One systematic category provided in the input products, NormDISCC, is identically zero; we carry

it as zero, which we have not been able to confirm is intended. No separate uncertainty is assigned
to the counting of protons on target: the NuMI flux prediction carries a 2 % counting term by
convention, so a separate band would risk counting it twice. Two links in that argument, that the
flux universes carry the term and that the neutrino–electron constraint absorbs it, are inferred, not
verified. The twelve playlists are combined with one global exposure ratio rather than per-period
ratios, and the resulting bias is not corrected. At reconstruction level the pooled result is high, by
0.18 % in total and by at most 0.32 % in a marginal bin. After unfolding, the pooled result is 0.076 %
above the exposure-weighted combination of the period results on the integral, the same sign as
at reconstruction level. It differs from that combination by at most 0.39 % in a marginal bin (the
highest p∥ bin) and by at most 0.33 % in pT . In single cells the combination differs from the pooled
result by −1.83 % to 3.78 %, each with |z| at most 0.31.
Other limitations. Both programs that use the covariance for the reported results, the χ2
comparison (§A.3.2) and the program that draws Fig. 4, check that its stored set of cells equals
the published set, and the check passes in every run of both on the quoted covariance. Limitations
specific to one block are stated with that block (§4.2, §4.3, §4.4, §6.3) and, for the duplicated pion
band, below.
Seed-related terms reported, not added. The variance of the bootstrap × seed interaction is a
per-bin median 0.072 of the statistical block’s variance. For the universe × retraining term, in the
six tested universes (of 187) with a non-null shift, the standard deviation of each universe’s shift
over training seeds, with four degrees of freedom, is a median 1.08–1.36 times the σ of the earlier
ten-seed block. In those universes the universe block, built at one training seed, therefore carries
single-seed training noise of about the earlier seed block’s size; it is part of the block as built and
is not separated from the physical variation. In two of them the noise is comparable to or larger
than the shift: in 2p2h:0 the shift is 0.097 % against a seed standard deviation of 0.192 %, and
in MaCCQE:0 0.239 % against 0.210 % (per-bin medians). We infer that in expectation such noise
adds variance to a band rather than removing it, and, from the medians, that these two bands are
largely training noise. The universe × retraining values and the band-centering numbers cannot be
recomputed from the saved covariance products; they were therefore not recomputed independently,
and their verification is limited to the checksums of their inputs.
Comparison with the earlier 2D budget. The saved products and code of the earlier 2D
budget [42] show the following defects in its construction; the quoted budget corrects each. First, the
systematic sweep of the earlier budget (the May sweep) has a simulated truth input that differs from
that of its seed-42 reference: even an EtaNCEL universe that leaves the event weights essentially
unchanged differs in all 205 truth-input cells, while its saved reconstructed inputs agree. A common
displacement cancels to first order in the MAT mean-centered covariance. The mismatch does not
invalidate the entire covariance, and it supplies no correction to subtract. Because the May input
and code revision are not available, the mismatch cannot be attributed more specifically. The July
sweep, which the quoted universe block uses, does not have this mismatch. Second, the two bands
Rvn1pi and Rvp1pi encode the same shift under the configured nonresonant-pion tune, and the
earlier budget counted it twice; the quoted universe block drops Rvp1pi and counts it once (§4.1).
The band is small, 0.088 %, but the double count is a defect in the construction of the covariance;
no conclusion about the published analysis’s implementation follows. Third, the earlier budget did
not propagate background-template statistics. In the saved reconstructed histograms their error
exceeds one tenth of the data’s Poisson error in 60 of 205 bins, so neglecting them everywhere is

unsupported; this is a reconstructed-space diagnostic, not a measured unfolded uncertainty. The
quoted statistical block draws the background simulation as its own bootstrap stream (§4.2), and
the quoted universe block takes its lateral bands from re-unfolds of re-selected universes (§4.1). The
values printed for the earlier budget were not changed by these findings.

4.7

What changes for the higher-dimensional products

Two event-loop modes supply the lateral bands. The single-pass event loop, which writes every
universe in one pass, stores each lateral universe’s shifted observables only for events that pass
the selection at the central value, so it omits events that the shift moves into the selection. Reselected event loops re-run the selection with one lateral universe active at a time, so they include
these migrations. For the five kinematic bands (beam angle BeamAngleX/Y, muon resolution
MuonResolution, and the muon energy scales Muon_Energy_MINERvA/MINOS), the re-selected
event loops supply the lateral-band block of CZ ; higher-dimensional products built from the single-pass
shifted observables are not used for any quoted uncertainty. Replacing the single-pass lateral-band
block by the re-selected one moves that block’s square-root trace by −0.0288 %. This aggregate
change does not bound the change in any individual bin. Across the 10 694 reported bins the per-bin
ratio of re-selected to single-pass standard deviation runs 0.177 to 3.062; within the 1 % of bins with
the largest single-pass variance, which carry 74.11 % of the total variance, it runs 0.687 to 1.153. A
small share of the aggregate variance does not make a bin irrelevant to a quoted result.
Scope of the re-selected replacement. The replacement covers the five detector bands using
shifted reconstructed kinematics. MINOS efficiency and the three GEANT hadron-interaction bands
(GEANT_Neutron, GEANT_Pion, GEANT_Proton) keep their weight-based treatment and are
therefore outside the kinematic replacement. This classification does not independently validate
hadronic-response model completeness for the Eavail and W measurement.
The ML term CML of CZ (Eq. (4)) is the split-varied band: its ensemble fixes the estimator seed
at 42 and varies the train/test split seed. Each of its replicas refits every classifier on a random
training subset of the events and still reweights every event, the only use of a training subset in this
note (training details: §3.1). The band therefore measures split response and not the full estimator
stochasticity. A separate scan of √
twelve estimator seeds at fixed data and split covers the remaining
stochasticity. Its covariance has Tr C = 1.306 × 10−39 cm2 /nucleon, which is 87.5 % of that of the
split-varied band (1.493 × 10−39 cm2 /nucleon). Estimator stochasticity, not the particular train/test
partition, therefore dominates the split-varied band. The scan is an auxiliary robustness check, not
an independent covariance term, since split and estimator responses are not assumed
√ uncorrelated.
Both bands are minor components, about three percent of the combined budget in Tr C.
CZ is a separate construction in which the unified-throw covariance is centered on the central
value (the CV-centered variant, cv; §9.6). A 5D covariance construction that preceded it used both
mean-centered and CV-centered unified throws (§4.5); neither variant is used, and no magnitude
from either is quoted.
Subtracting the background separately in each of the 187 universe unfolds and in the CV changes
the square-root trace of the combined block sum by 0.28 % and CZ by 0.18 %, a numerically negligible
bound on the background-CV choice (Ref. [37], superseded values). The 4D and extended-fiducial
results have no covariance from this procedure, and CZ is not transferred to them.

Validation of the two-dimensional measurement

In a simulated experiment, truth is the known target distribution, the prior is the starting simulated
truth distribution, and the response describes reconstruction and selection conditional on truth. A
closure test compares the unfolded estimate with its known target under a specified deformation.
Bias is the mean estimate minus that target over repeated experiments; a single closure residual also
contains sampling fluctuations. Coverage is the fraction of repeated intervals containing the specified
truth, and tests the estimator and interval procedure together. Table 4 separates these questions
before the results. Repeating a numerical calculation checks neither the coverage and width of the
intervals nor the adequacy of the physical model.

LightGBM and five iterations; pass thresholds are in parentheses.
• Truth reweighting (the same factor on truth and reco weights): a Gaussian in pT and a tilt
in p∥ leave residuals of 0.046 % and 0.013 %, within the 1.5 % threshold.

the leakage is linear in amplitude, A = 0.05/0.10/0.30 → 0.57/1.14/3.43 % (threshold 3.0 %);
the first two amplitudes pass, and the deliberately large A = 0.30 arm exceeds the threshold.
• Alternative model (training on the central-value simulation, pseudo-data from an alternative

Injected-feature recovery. The 2D truth-reweighting closure with a Gaussian bump passes the
recovery test of the unbinned-unfolding guide [18]. In the feature bins the residual from the injected
truth is 1.69 % for an injected 17.2 % feature (ratio 0.098), about ten times smaller. The test requires

the residual left after recovering a deviation from the prior to be far smaller than the deviation itself;
unfolding cannot create discriminating power (3D: §7.4).
A data-versus-prior χ2 with a diagonal covariance of statistical variances only is larger at truth
level than at reco level. This is expected at high resolution: detector smearing lowers the reco-level
value, and the diagonal covariance omits the unfolding covariance. The comparisons that use a full
covariance are the χ2 against the published result (§6.4) and the generator comparisons.
These closure tests validate the estimator; no non-closure uncertainty is assigned from them.
Assigning one would require a predeclared suite of injection shapes and amplitudes, not calibration
to a single Gaussian bump.
None of these tests uses pseudo-data whose detector response differs from the unfolding response.
Appendix D defines such a test, a closure that rescales only the reconstructed Eavail of the pseudodata, together with a more complete leading-nucleon follow-up. No result from it is reported here.
The five-dimensional estimator, including its background subtraction, is validated in §9.1.
Requirements for validated total uncertainties. The follow-up assessment [42] distinguishes
a proposed split-sample width check from repeated-sampling coverage. With the MC sample,
binning maps and background subtraction held fixed, such a check could establish only the precision
conditional on them, at its tested exposure. Nonlinear unfolding does not guarantee a simple
scaling from half to full exposure. Nor can the check supply the variation across independent MC
populations. With the current inputs, validated total uncertainties for all 205 bins, the integral
and the projections therefore cannot be obtained. They would require independent populations
or a validated generative law, a continuous nuisance generator for tests of total intervals, and an
untouched generator-truth domain for bias checks (§4.6). This is a limit of the available inputs, not
a general impossibility result for 2D unfolding.

5.1

Closure tests across dimensions

Only the MINERvA simulation supplies a detector response, the conditional distribution of reconstructed events given truth. Other generators (GiBUU, NuWro, GENIE variants) supply truth
only. Every closure test here therefore keeps the simulated MINERvA response and changes the
truth distribution, by reweighting the simulated events, or changes the analysis chain around it.
Reweighting to another generator’s shape is a valid known-truth test of the unfolding; it is not a
test of the detector model. An ordinary closure, whose pseudo-data are the simulation itself, is an
identity check that a constant estimator would also pass. Table 5 lists, for each test, what it changes
relative to the unfolding’s simulation and what its result can detect.

Comparison of injected-bump and generator-shaped closure tests. The injected-bump
and generator-shaped tests differ in deformation, metric and iteration dependence, so their results
do not contradict each other.
• Deformation. The closing injections are single smooth bumps in one variable the classifiers
see directly. A generator ratio changes correlated structure across Eavail , W and q3 , including directions the reconstructed observables constrain weakly (§9.5, response-information
diagnostic).
• Metric. The 3D, 4D and 5D closures are judged on one-dimensional marginals, where per-cell
errors average out; the 3D test’s per-bin spread is already 3.2 %, and the 5D test’s is 3.7 %.
The generator-shaped failures are measured in joint (Eavail , W ) cells.
• Iterations. At five iterations the estimator retains part of the prior wherever the reconstructed
data constrain the truth weakly. Thirty iterations reduce the Eavail residual by 26 % while the
q3 residual grows (§9.3).
• Five-dimensional W test. A W bump at the ∆ peak is recovered to a ratio of 0.180 against a
threshold of 0.20, a narrow pass in the marginal only. The same unfolding result leaves joint
(Eavail , W ) cells in the peak column up to 9.4 % low and moves part of the bump into the
neighbouring W bins. A single smooth bump in a marginal is therefore weaker evidence than
the generator-shaped tests, which remain the main five-dimensional recovery evidence of §9.

5.2

Fixed-truth coverage of the 2D statistical band

This test asks whether a 2D statistical band covers a fixed truth at its nominal rate. The test scored
the completeness-divided band, the first-built statistical band, quoted when the test ran, whose
replicas divide by a bootstrapped completeness. The undivided band is the rebuilt band, without
the division, since superseded. The quoted statistical block (§4.2) is a separate ensemble of 300
replicas at the central value’s training seed. The design, criteria and positive control of the test were
specified before any toy was generated (Ref. [37], R1.1).
Each toy draws pseudo-data event by event as Poisson counts with means equal to the simulated
reconstructed weights. It resamples the simulation with the per-event Poisson(1) bootstrap of the
statistical band and unfolds with that band’s estimator, LightGBM with five iterations at a fixed
training seed, the learner of the quoted central value at a different seed (§3.2). The reference T is
the unfluctuated simulated truth, identical in every toy. The no-fluctuation configuration reproduces
both the production closure output and T exactly.
In reported bin b of toy t, ztb = (Utb − Tb )/(rb Tb ), where rb is the production band’s relative
spread. Coverage Ck is the fraction of all (toy, bin) pairs with |z| ≤ k, with intervals from resampling
whole toys. The band counts as nominal if its ratio to the true scatter lies in [0.9, 1.1], which gives
the windows C1 ∈ [0.632, 0.729] and C2 ∈ [0.928, 0.972]. A failure at the interim analysis of 100 toys,
with 99.5% intervals, stops the study.
At the interim analysis (Ntoy = 100, 205 bins) the completeness-divided band undercovers in its
tails. Its one-standard-deviation coverage C1 = 0.679 [0.669, 0.690] is nominal (Gaussian 0.683), but
its two-standard-deviation coverage C2 = 0.912 [0.907, 0.918] lies entirely below its window (Gaussian
0.954). The pull RMS is 1.60, and the mean pull is consistent with zero. By the criteria above
this is a failure by undercoverage, and the study stopped at the interim analysis. An independent
reimplementation of the scoring reproduces the pooled coverages, their intervals, the controls and
the secondary analysis below.

The positive control rescales the band on the same toys. At ×0.7 and ×1.3, C2 moves to 0.809
and 0.955, and the test flags both directions.
The failure is one of width, not bias. The per-bin pattern, which is descriptive and post hoc, is
uneven in both directions. The band is too wide in 82 bins (pull RMS below 0.9) and too narrow in
71 (above 1.1). The pooled C1 is nominal partly through this cancellation; the median bin’s pull
RMS is 0.97. 35 bins have a per-bin C2 below 0.85. Of these, 21 form a block at p∥ ≥ 15 GeV/c
and pT < 0.55 GeV/c. Six more lie in the highest-pT row (2.5 GeV/c to 4.5 GeV/c) at p∥ 6 GeV/c
to 20 GeV/c. The 14 bins with pull RMS above 2 carry 72 % of the C2 deficit.
Unlike the band’s replicas, the central value has completeness exactly 1. A secondary analysis,
added after the pilot, applies the band’s division to the toys. Scored against the same band, the
divided toys overcover: C1 = 0.833, with its interval above the window. The attribution rule was
fixed before the full run, but its clause for the undercoverage outcome was written after the pilot
pulls had been seen. Under this rule the primary undercoverage is attributed to the completeness
term of the band. The attribution is partial: in the replica form, the p∥ 40 GeV/c to 60 GeV/c
column still undercovers.
The band was then rebuilt without this term. The resulting undivided band was rescored only
post hoc (below), and the quoted statistical block has not been tested.
Post hoc rescoring against the undivided band. Scoring the same 100 toys against
the undivided band removes the tail undercoverage: C2 = 0.977 [0.974, 0.980] and C1 = 0.781
[0.771, 0.790], both above their nominal windows, so the undivided band is wider than the toy scatter
(pull RMS 0.84, mean pull consistent with zero). The rescoring is post hoc and partly circular,
because the band’s MC part reuses the toys’ resampling mechanism.
The excess width lies in the data-statistics component. The per-bin pull RMS falls as that
component’s share of the band’s variance rises, which corresponds to a data-statistics part about
1.6× wider in σ than the toys produce. Background purity does not explain it, and its cause is not
established. A post hoc diagnostic bootstraps a single toy; its decision rule was recorded before it
ran. Its result is consistent with the closure toys scattering too little, rather than with the data
bootstrap scattering too much. The toys would scatter too little because their pseudo-data are built
on the training MC events. The diagnostic is neither an independent-population coverage test nor a
calibration on real data. A re-test on held-out simulation would separate the two explanations; it
has not been run.
Scope. The test concerns the statistical band alone and changes no central value or quoted
uncertainty. It tests the LightGBM statistical estimator at the band’s training seed (seed 1), not
at the quoted central value’s seed 42, and does not address systematic, total or five-dimensional
coverage. Its simulation-side scatter reuses the resampling that built the band, so only the data-side
fluctuation is tested by an independent mechanism. Because T equals the prior, the test cannot
detect regularization bias toward the prior.

5.3

Held-out-tune coverage of the 2D covariance

A second coverage test, declared before it ran, asks whether the analysis procedure’s 2D covariance
covers truths held out of its construction. At each of two held-out GENIE v3 tunes, AR23_20i_00_000 and G18_10a_02_11b, with 300 pseudo-experiments each and the analysis procedure’s
2D covariance remade at each tune’s truth (model term included, run-period term excluded), the
declared test reads under-covers. The joint in-region count is 0 of 300 at both tunes, against declared
thresholds of 190 (AR23_20i_00_000) and 192 (G18_10a_02_11b). Per cell, two cells are flagged
at AR23_20i_00_000 and one at G18_10a_02_11b, each attributed to bias (Table 6). The mean
χ2 /ndf of the pseudo-experiments is 16.55 ± 0.17 (ndf = 204) at AR23_20i_00_000 and 24.17 ± 0.37

(ndf = 205) at G18_10a_02_11b. The test’s false-flag rate under its null’s law is at most 0.0348
and 0.0363 per tune.
