so CV centering retains the mean shift as variance and mean centering removes it. The covariance
used here is the
√ CV-centered variant, which retains the shift deliberately; the mean-centered variant
(mean) has a TrC lower by 7.13 %. A file’s variant is identified by its metadata, not by its path.
The projection to (Eavail , W ). The quoted 42-cell covariance is CEW = M CZ M ⊤ , with M of
shape 42 × 10 694 over kept axes (Eavail , W ). Each entry of M is the product of the dropped axes’
bin widths, giving a differential density in cm2 /GeV2 /nucleon. Integrating a destination cell requires
its own ∆Eavail ∆W . Rows follow the 7 × 6 (Eavail , W ) grid in measured C order. No source cell
is dropped, and the result is exactly symmetric. Support and pinned masks and row-index arrays
accompany the covariance and are byte-identical in the two variants. The covariance is paired with
the central values of Fig. 24 by checksum: the central-value array of the projected source and that
of the figure are byte-identical, with numerical agreement only as a cross-check.
Conditioning, and why no rank is quoted. Measured extremes λmin = +4.359104 × 10−92
and λmax = 1.488215 × 10−77 give λmin /λmax = 2.9 × 10−15 and condition number ≈ 3.4 × 1014 , at
double precision’s edge. No eigenvalue is negative, but the smallest lie at the floating-point noise

floor of a 10 694-term weighted sum. Their sign carries no information: nnegative = 0 describes this
computation and does not show that the source’s negative directions were cured. Rank is a property
of the cutoff, not of the matrix. The 42 eigenvalues decay smoothly across about fifteen orders of
magnitude with no plateau. Retained counts are 26 at relative cutoff 10−6 , 36 at the code default
10−12 , 41 at numpy.linalg.matrix_rank’s default nϵ = 9.3 × 10−15 , and 42 without a cutoff. No
rank is therefore quoted for this covariance; in particular, 36 is the count at one cutoff, not a number
of degrees of freedom. An inverse requires a declared regularization, and none is applied implicitly.
What the covariance supports. Four measurements limit what the covariance supports. Items
1, 2 and 4 use two companion covariances built at two estimator-seed settings; only item 3 describes
this file.
1. Estimator-seed sensitivity exceeds its limit. Companion covariances at seed offsets {0, 1200}
give the correlation-sensitive measure sproj = 6.145 %, against the movement limit of 5 %,
fixed on 2026-09-18 before production as a direct tolerance at the scale of the ensemble’s own
precision. Ap160-throw ensemble determines a standard deviation only to a relative precision
of about 1/ 2(N − 1) ≈ 5.6 % for independent Gaussian throws, so a smaller seed-induced
drift could not be resolved against the width it would change. The limit is a declared stability
tolerance, not a derived confidence threshold for a maximum over correlated projections. The
diagonal-only measures pass (sagg = 0.471 %, smed = 0.485 %); the failing one probes the
off-diagonal structure used by projections.
2. Within the limits stated below, the sensitivity is not the statistic’s own resampling noise,
which would fall with N . With the throw ensemble fixed and only the seed changed, the
sensitivity does not fall over the tested ensemble sizes: mean 6.02 % over four disjoint 40-throw
subsets, 6.02 % over two 80-throw halves, and 6.145 % over all N = 160 throws. The same-seed
resampling floor falls steeply from 20.91 % at N = 40 to 7.57 % at N = 80, with two-point
exponent 1.467; no floor measurement exists at N = 160. Three limits rule out a statement
about infinite ensembles. First, the 40- and 80-throw points are nested subsets of the same
160 throws, not independent ensembles. The flatness therefore holds within one ensemble, over
a factor of four in N ; the behaviour at larger N is unmeasured. Second, the quoted ±0.39 %
is subset scatter at a single seed pair (offsets {0, 1200}); the width of the distribution over
seed pairs is unmeasured. Third, the statistic is a maximum over the declared offset set, so
additional pairs can only raise it.
3. Five of the covariance’s 45 bands fall outside the seed variation. The endpoint convention
fixes the estimator seed for the muon-energy, muon-resolution
and beam-angle blocks, so the
√
offset cannot reach them. They carry 26.0 % of Tr C and move by exactly zero; their seed
sensitivity is unmeasured. Allowing them to vary could move the total in either direction, and
neither is measured, because sproj maximizes relative changes over functionals rather than
summing nonnegative component contributions.
4. Seed shifts in central values are a small fraction of their uncertainty. Across the comparison’s 43
projection functionals (42 destinations plus the all-ones total rate), shifts reach 0.761 %, versus
relative uncertainties of order ten percent. They reach at most 6.02 % of quoted uncertainty,
with median 1.06 %. The largest shift occurs in the destination cell driving the covariance
result. Individual five-axis bins reach 49.8 % of their uncertainty; summing bins suppresses
this movement in projections.

The estimator-seed magnitude criterion specified before production has not been computed for this
covariance. It was computed only for the two companion covariances built for that purpose, and
their outcomes do not transfer to this covariance (Ref. [37], R1.4).

10

Illustrative sixteen-cell five-observable output on data

This section gives the five-dimensional output of configuration (e) (§3.2) on the data, on 16 cells, R00–
R15, with total uncertainties from the reported covariance Ctotal + Cresp + CPL,5D . It is illustrative
output, not for fitting or for constraining models: no interval has validated coverage, the fake-data
bias exceeds the stated criteria at 2 truths (§10.5), the cells R02, R06, R10 and R15 have low
reconstruction stability (§10.1), the run-period block was added after the period differences were
seen (§10.4), and the cells depend on the unfolding algorithm (§10.6). It is a separate product from
the configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value of Secs. 8 and 9 and from the five-axis covariance
used with that central value (§9.6). Statements in those sections about that central value and that
covariance do not describe this result, and statements here do not describe them. The section gives
the cells and the result (§10.1), the result’s quadrant sums against the lower-dimensional results
(§10.2), the covariance and what it is built from (§10.3), the fake-data tests of the estimator’s bias
against the covariance (§10.5), the dependence on the unfolding algorithm (§10.6) and the descriptive
comparison with generator predictions (§10.7).

10.1

Cells and result

The cells are formed from the H2 grid of §9.4, which divides each axis once into 32 boxes, with edges
pT = (0, 0.7, 4.5), p∥ = (1.5, 4.5, 60), Eavail = (0, 0.8, 100), q3 = (0, 2, 100) and W = (0, 1.8, 100), in
the units of App. B.2. Of these, 27 meet that appendix’s support rule (minimum expected signal
and truth weight). Each supported box with purity below τ = 0.1 is merged into a neighbour, in 12
predefined steps. The merges absorb 1 unsupported box into cell R09; the other 4 are not reported.
Each of the 16 resulting cells meets the support rule. Four of them, R02, R06, R10 and R15, have a
reconstruction stability of 0.16–0.25 (the other twelve, 0.26–0.81): of the simulated signal events
generated in such a cell and reconstructed, that fraction is reconstructed in the same cell. No
stability criterion is applied in forming the cells. Table 13 gives each cell’s box, its cell-integrated
cross section f and its relative uncertainty.

10.2

Quadrant sums against the lower-dimensional results

The sixteen cells form four (pT , p∥ ) quadrants, divided at pT = 0.7 and p∥ = 4.5 GeV/c, of four cells
each. In each quadrant the sum S16 of the sixteen-cell result is compared with the sum S2D of the
adopted two-dimensional result over the same region, through d = S16 − S2D . On the sixteen-cell side
the covariance is the reported one; on the two-dimensional side it is the four-block covariance of §4.5
in the first comparison below, and that covariance with its generator-shape term Cmodel in the second.
The two results share the data, the flux and most systematic variations, and come from different
estimators: configuration (e) for the sixteen cells and configuration (f) for the two-dimensional result
(§3.2). The three-observable marginal of configuration (e), compared with the two-dimensional result
over all 205 reported cells, agrees with it to 0.141 % in area-weighted total (§7.3); that is a different
quantity from the quadrant differences below, of which QA’s is +0.141 %.

With the cross covariance. The second comparison evaluates the covariance between the two
results from what they share, and tests the four differences jointly. The cross covariance sums paired
terms: 34 of the 38 systematic bands, universe by universe, after their signal weights were checked to
agree event by event between the two analyses; the 5 lateral shifts; the normalization; the run-period
terms, paired period by period by construction; and the Cmodel shifts, paired generator by generator.
Two parts could not be paired. The statistical correlation is not measured: the paired bootstrap
replicas give cross correlations that do not fit the statistical blocks of the two results, which were

built from other replica sets, and the joint matrix they form is not positive semidefinite. The weight
check failed in AGKYxF1pi, AhtBY, BhtBY, MinosEfficiency, on non-finite ratios only, between the
1/2 1/2
two analyses’ universe-to-central weight ratios. These five enter as r S16 S2D , with S each side’s
own block, for r ∈ [0, 1]: a one-parameter family that keeps the joint covariance positive semidefinite,
not every admissible correlation. The rule, fixed in advance, is that the results agree if the joint χ2
of the four differences has p ≥ 0.05 and every |z| ≤ 2.50; it is read at every point of a 10 001-point
grid in r, and 99 structural and block-by-block controls passed. Table 15 gives the result. The two
results agree at every r and in every reading. In the primary reading χ2 = 1.170 (p = 0.883) at
r = 0 and 1.240 (p = 0.871) at r = 1, and the largest |z| is 0.985, in QC at r = 1. Each |z| is largest
at an end of the family, since its variance is linear in r, and χ2 is convex in r, so it is largest, and
p smallest, at an end; the value of χ2 between the ends is not recorded. The implied correlation
between the two quadrant sums is 0.77–0.92 at r = 0 and 0.96–0.99 at r = 1.

Summed over each of the four (pT , p∥ ) quadrants divided at pT = 0.7 and p∥ = 4.5 GeV/c, the
sixteen-cell result lies 0.2–0.8 % below the three-observable central value of §7, the largest difference
being 0.17 of the sixteen-cell uncertainty of the quadrant sum. This comparison is descriptive, not
a test: the three-observable central value carries no uncertainty here, since no 3D covariance is
adopted, so it reads as agreement or disagreement in neither direction. The two quadrants with
pT < 0.7 GeV/c include unreported boxes at their central value, a share of at most 1.1 × 10−7 of the
sum.

10.3

The reported covariance

The reported covariance is Ctotal + Cresp + CPL,5D , with Ctotal = Ce + Cmodel ; the run-period block
CPL,5D is described in §10.4. Ce sums the statistical bootstrap of configuration (e), 50 replicas,
each of which refits the whole unfolding with its own rounding-level perturbation of the inputs; the
PPFX flux universes, weighted by the neutrino–electron and inverse-muon-decay constraint; the
interaction-model and detector bands, which reweight simulated events; the lateral bands, which
shift reconstructed kinematics (§4); the normalization; and the training-seed block. With full-sample
binning and deterministic training, the unfolds at the other seeds are bitwise equal to the central

one, so the measured training-seed block is zero in every cell. Table 16 gives each group’s per-cell
relative σ.
For Cmodel , the prior is reweighted in turn by the ratio of GENIE + MEC, NuWro or GiBUU to
GENIE CV, and the data are unfolded again. Each generator’s shift is the relative change of the
data result, and Cmodel is the unscaled sum of their outer products. It is generator-shape model
dependence, not a calibrated bound; its coverage at truths held out of its construction has not
been validated. The GiBUU reweighting is applied only below p∥ = 10 GeV/c; above it the prior
is unchanged. Between p∥ = 6 and 10 GeV/c, the GiBUU sample lacks the contribution from
Eν > 20 GeV, which is at least 4.9–5.3% of the cross section there in the GENIE CV and NuWro
samples (which stop at Eν = 50 GeV). The GiBUU shift contributes 0.890 of the trace of Cmodel , the
NuWro shift 0.095 and the GENIE + MEC shift 0.014. In every cell, 77–100% of the prior weight
lies next to a discontinuity larger than 0.1 in the reweighting ratio, created by the ratio’s statistical
gate or by a cut; the shifts are used unmodified.
The coverage of Cmodel is not validated because an earlier route to a calibrated model term with
validated coverage did not pass its first two tests and was not pursued further, after which Cmodel
was adopted unscaled (the J and H2 cells and estimator R are those of §9.4). The first, Stage 0,
tested that earlier term on the J and H2 cells, not Cmodel on the sixteen cells. It asked whether a
model term built from the data result’s shifts under generator reweightings of the prior, with its scale
calibrated on estimator R’s bias at the truth deformations assigned to the other generator families,
covers that bias, cell by cell, at a family held out of the calibration, requiring a covered fraction of at
least 0.60 at every held-out family and of at least 0.68 pooled over the families. This first part failed
on both sets of cells: on the H2 cells, whose grid the sixteen cells are formed from, GiBUU reached
0.259, although the pooled 0.697 met its bar; on the J cells, NuWro reached 0.570 and GiBUU
0.541, and the pooled 0.645 did not meet its bar. Its second part, on the term’s size and on whether
truth departures stay distinguishable from the nominal with the term in the covariance, passed on
both. With the first part failed, Stage 0 stopped. The second test, a reconstruction-information test
on the J cells, asked whether more reconstructed information, from finer reconstructed binning or
added reconstructed variables, narrows a width floor and reduces the residual of a binned iterative
unfolding at truth departures. None of its 11 configurations met its residual requirement, and the
test ended incomplete, neither passing nor stopping.
For Cresp , the simulation’s reconstructed recoil energy is scaled by one standard deviation
separately for four deposit categories: protons, mesons, electromagnetic deposits and other deposits.
Cresp sums the covariance contributions of the four resulting shifts of configuration (e)’s data result.
It does not cover neutron or muon deposits, the effects of particle species, energy dependence and
resolution beyond a scale, the null distributions of the joint tests, the configuration-(a) products or
the two-, three- and four-dimensional results. The median per-cell relative σ of Cresp is 2.14%, and
adding Cresp to Ctotal raises the per-cell σ by a factor of 1.011–1.220 (median 1.048). A cross-talk
term, reported beside Cresp and not summed into it, has a median per-cell relative σ of 0.36%. Of
the trace of (Ctotal + Cresp )/f f T , Ctotal contributes 0.9262 and the four deposit categories 0.0168,
0.0436, 0.0071 and 0.0062.
Table 17 gives the correlation matrix of the reported Ctotal + Cresp + CPL,5D ; with the central
values and the relative uncertainties of Table 13, it gives the reported covariance to the printed
precision.
Every χ2 of this section inverts the full covariance of the cells compared, 16 × 16 or 8 × 8 for
GiBUU’s cells, by Cholesky decomposition and without truncation, so the inverted dimension p
equals the number of cells and is fixed in advance; the shape-only χ2 inverts the same matrix with
100 f f T added and has one degree of freedom fewer. The blocks inverted are of unlike kinds. Two are
sample covariances of an ensemble: the statistical block, from 50 bootstrap replicas with the unbiased

1/(N − 1) normalization, and the flux block, from 100 PPFX universes with constraint weights,
normalized by the sum of the weights (the weighted analogue of 1/N ; effective number of universes
19). The 2p2h band has three universes. The other bands and the lateral shifts are two-point shifts,
the normalization is one outer product, Cmodel sums three chosen generator shifts, Cresp four ±1σ
shifts, and CPL,5D is built from three exposure-weighted periods; the training-seed block is zero.
Each comparison with a prediction adds Vg , its diagonal sum of squared event weights, and each
fake-data comparison the variance of the mean over its pseudo-experiments. No finite-ensemble
correction is applied to any block. The standard correction is derived for a single unweighted sample
covariance, which none of these sums is; the flux bootstrap below measures the scatter of the χ2
from estimating the flux block, and is not a correction.

10.4

The run-period block

On the sixteen cells, the unfolded results of the three run-period groups (playlists 1A–1F, 1G–1M
and 1N–1P), each over its own simulated truth (the double ratio), differ from one another by more
than the scatter of their bootstrap replicas. The offsets of the twelve playlists differ between the
three period groups by more than expected from their playlist-to-playlist scatter. The cause is not
known, and these data cannot establish which period, if any, is right. As for the two-dimensional
result (§4.5, Table 3), the block CPL,5D is built from these double ratios with no reference period:
each group’s offset from the exposure-weighted mean of the three, applied to the central value, with
a reliability-weight factor for the weighted spread. It has rank 2 and is not debiased for the replica
noise it contains. With three groups its own size is poorly determined: the 68 % interval on the ratio
of its true to its estimated σ is 0.73–2.56, and in cells R00, R08 it is dominated by that noise.
Each of the twelve playlists was also unfolded on its own, with its own simulation and bootstrap
replicas, in a study whose tests, bounds and readings were fixed before any playlist result was read.
A playlist’s offset is its double ratio on the sixteen cells relative to the exposure-weighted mean
of the twelve. The statistic F of a division of the playlists into groups is the share of the offsets’
exposure-weighted squared size, measured with the inverse of the relative Ctotal + Cresp , that the
groups’ exposure-weighted mean offsets carry. For the three run-period groups F = 0.70, the largest
of all 9,240 divisions of the twelve playlists into groups of six, three and three, so the permutation
p-value is 1/9,240 = 1.08 × 10−4 , the smallest the test can give. The permutation is approximate:
the playlists differ in exposure and hence in noise, so their labels are not exchangeable, and the
test’s validity rests on a simulated null. In each of 2,000 simulated datasets the playlists’ offsets
are drawn independently from one covariance, estimated from the twelve playlists and scaled by 0,
0.5, 1 or 2, with each playlist’s own bootstrap and simulation noise added; the share of datasets
with p < 0.05 is 0.0395–0.0565 over the four scales, within the bound of 0.0646. For an alternative
chronological division into groups of five, two, two and three playlists (1A–1E, 1F and 1G, 1L and
1M, and 1N–1P), the same statistic gives p = 5.17 × 10−3 over its 83,160 divisions; this comparison
is descriptive.
In the form of the two-dimensional test (§4.5), the five-observable unfolds of the run-period
groups give the double ratio of 1G–1M to 1A–1F a p∥ step of (6.28 ± 0.60) % across 8 GeV/c, 10.5
times its bootstrap scatter. Each playlist’s own step, against 1A–1F (leaving the playlist out when it
is one of them), was classed as consistent with none within two standard deviations (FREE), larger
than half of the 1G–1M group’s step, recombined from its three playlists, by two standard deviations
(CARRIES), or neither (PARTIAL). The classes are CARRIES for 1L and 1M; PARTIAL for 1G,
1N and 1P; and FREE for 1A–1F and 1O. The study’s two patterns are that all three of 1G, 1L
and 1M carry the step, or that some carry it and the others are free, each with 1A–1F free. Neither
holds, so no statement of where the step lies is drawn from these classes. None of these readings

identifies a cause.
The block was chosen after the period differences had been seen. Before it was added, an audit
examined its overlap with the existing blocks, especially the flux and detector-response ones; its noise
treatment, with bootstrap replicas of each period; why its cell pattern differs from the projection
of the two-dimensional period offsets, although their sizes agree; and the stability of its rank-two
construction. The audit’s stopping condition was fixed before any of its numbers were read, and
it did not fire. The addition therefore followed the data. Passing the audit establishes neither the
block’s size nor the absence of overlap: descriptively, one detector-response pair, a pion-interaction
band with the meson response shift, reproduces one period’s offset pattern within noise and part of
the other two. Built from the observed differences, the block contains them by construction, and its
size and coverage have not been independently validated. The per-cell uncertainties, the fake-data
tests and the generator comparison are given here with and without it.
Added to Ctotal + Cresp , the block raises the median per-cell relative σ from 7.29 % to 7.86 %, by
about 7.8 %. The median of the cells’ own increases is 4.8 %, and the largest is 14.1 %, in R04. The
block’s median per-cell relative σ is 2.34 %, at most 3.16 %, and its share of the reported variance has
a median of 9.0 % over the cells and a maximum of 23.2 %. With the block, each prediction’s total
χ2 is lower by 3.6–29.3 % of its value without it, and each shape-only χ2 by 7.4–40.4 % (Table 20).
We infer from these falls that the predictions’ residuals have components along the directions the
rank-2 block adds; the comparison does not test this interpretation. Under the reported covariance
one fake-data truth fewer is listed (§10.5), with the bias unchanged.

10.5

Fake-data tests of the estimator’s bias

The fake-data tests compare the estimator’s bias at truths other than the simulation with the
reported covariance. Pseudo-data are generated at 12 truths and unfolded with configuration (e).
The mean unfolded result is compared with each truth on the 16 cells, through χ2 with 16 degrees of
freedom and the per-cell pull z. The truths are the simulation’s prior; the five hybrid truths of the
generator tests (Sec. 11), each a prediction on the fine grid; the historical shapes W1, W2 and W3
of §9.4; a GiBUU-to-GENIE Eavail shape; a noise-free closure at the NuWro shape; and a synthetic
tilt of the q3 distribution at fixed (Eavail , W ). A truth from a Cmodel generator is compared with the
covariance without that generator’s shift. A truth is listed as failing when p < 0.01 or when any cell
has |z| > 2; these criteria were fixed before any of the tests was graded against them. These are
not held-out truths, and the detector response is not probed. Table 18 gives each truth under the
reported covariance, without CPL,5D , and under Ctotal alone. The bias of each truth is the same in
all three; only the covariance differs.
Under the reported covariance, 2 truths meet the listing criterion (p < 0.01, or a cell with |z| > 2):
the GiBUU hybrid truth, by p and by its cells R02, R06; and the q3 tilt, by p alone (p = 9.1 × 10−6 ).
Without CPL,5D , 3 truths meet it: the GiBUU Eavail shape is also listed, by its cell R06 (z = 2.01,
against 1.76 with the block), and the GiBUU hybrid truth by its cells R02, R06, R07. The fake-data
bias therefore exceeds the stated thresholds at the GiBUU hybrid truth and the q3 tilt under the
reported covariance, and also at the GiBUU Eavail shape without CPL,5D ; the bias is the same in
both, so the difference is the covariance’s, not a smaller bias or a validated uncertainty. At the other
10 truths the bias meets both criteria under the reported covariance. Two validity rules classify each
cell from these tests; neither is a coverage test, and no cell is excluded or changed by them. Under
the ATLAS User Guide’s thresholds applied per cell (V-ATLAS), 14 cells are valid, every cell except
R02 and R06, where the GiBUU hybrid truth alone gives z = 4.19 and 4.53. The guide states no
rule comparing the bias spread with the spread of the predictions, so the second rule (V-PRIOR) is
ours: a cell is valid when the spread of its fake-data biases is below 0.5 of the spread of the generator

The truths form three groups, evaluated separately, not pooled: the 8 truths from the Cmodel
generators; the other truths, the GENIE CV hybrid and the q3 tilt; and the prior truths, the
simulation prior and the Tune v1 hybrid. Under the reported covariance, the Cmodel -generator truths
have 2 of 128 cells with |z| > 2 (Gaussian reference 5.82) and 1 of 8 truths with p < 0.01 (reference
0.08); the other truths 0 of 32 and 1 of 2; and the prior truths 0 of 32 and 0 of 2. The Gaussian
references are context only, because the bias is deterministic.
One control check failed: the offset check, which compares the offset corrections of two hybrid
truths, gives χ2 = 245.74 for 16 degrees of freedom. The other control checks (numbers 0, 1, 2, 3, 4,
5, 6, 8) passed. As fixed in advance, the six power alternatives built on that correction are therefore
descriptive and not judged against the failure criteria; Table 19 gives them, with two descriptive
variants of each tested truth.

Two truths are held out of the analysis: the GENIE v3 tunes G18_10a_02_11b and AR23_76

20i_00_000, generated for this test. Each tune enters as its normalized shape ratio to GENIE CV
on the fine grid, constructed as the reweighting ratios of the Cmodel shifts are. Where the tune’s
sample fails the ratio’s statistical gate, the ratio is pooled over a coarser cell or set to one. The
ratio is applied to the simulated truth, under the simulation’s detector response. At each tune,
20 Poisson pseudo-experiments on half the simulation and a noise-free closure are tested against
the full reported covariance, as truths with no shift in Cmodel , in a fourth group, not pooled with
the others. The bias meets both criteria in all four records, the pseudo-experiment mean and the
closure at each tune. The mean of the pseudo-experiments gives χ2 = 9.26 (p = 0.90; largest |z|
0.61, in R11) at G18_10a_02_11b and 14.06 (p = 0.59; 0.61, in R07) at AR23_20i_00_000; the
closures give 9.14 (p = 0.91; 0.60, in R11) and 13.43 (p = 0.64; 0.57, in R07). In every cell of the
four records, the bias is below 0.62 of the cell’s reported σ; in cell R11 at G18_10a_02_11b, the
bias lies below the reported uncertainty only with Cmodel included. This group has 0 of 64 cells
with |z| > 2 (Gaussian reference 2.91) and 0 of 4 records with p < 0.01 (reference 0.04). The four
records are the pseudo-experiment and closure results for two truths, so the Gaussian references,
which count them as independent, are context only.
The two tunes use the same quasielastic cross-section model, GENIE’s NievesQELCCPXSec,
AR23_20i_00_000 with a z-expansion form factor, and the MEC model of G18_10a_02_11b
is of the Valencia family used in the GENIE + MEC shift. Both are GENIE tunes, so they are
partial evidence and not independent tests. Descriptively, without the GENIE + MEC shift, the
pseudo-experiments give χ2 = 11.55 (p = 0.77) and 18.79 (p = 0.28), and the closures 11.07 (p = 0.81)
and 17.22 (p = 0.37). The model-only χ2 , defined as in Table 19, are 817–922 in the four records,
with cell R00 beyond 2σ at AR23_20i_00_000 in both its records. Rerunning the tests with the two
tunes added reproduces every earlier fake-data result exactly. The test is a bias test, not a coverage
test: its Poisson pseudo-experiments do not draw the systematic parameters, so they cannot test the
interval widths, and the detector response is not probed. A coverage test at these two tunes, declared
in advance for the covariance without the run-period block, with pseudo-experiments that also draw
the flux, interaction and detector uncertainties (the lateral and normalization ones as surrogates,
and Cmodel and Cresp not at all), was stopped before any coverage pseudo-experiment ran. At one of
its gates, the ratio of each cell’s uncertainty reproduced through the pseudo-experiments to that of
the drawn covariance lay outside 0.9–1.1 in 11 of the 16 cells, ranging from 0.70 to 2.30. Replacing
the pseudo-experiments’ values by the covariance’s for any three or fewer of the 38 drawn bands
does not bring every cell inside that window. For the interaction uncertainties, a later investigation
at the nominal truth found that the gate compared unlike objects. Its pseudo-experiments reproduce
in every cell the spread of the response alone, with the simulated truth held fixed, whereas the full
interaction universes from which the covariance is built give a different spread at the same truth. As
a sensitivity, the standard deviation of the response alone, carried into the data’s frame, is a median
1.65 times that of the reported interaction block (range 0.42–6.50 over the cells); it depends on the
fine-cell grid and is not a bound. Separately, for the interaction uncertainties, in all but one cell the
covariance’s construction gives a different spread at the nominal truth than at the data, where the
covariance is made. Pseudo-data that take each full interaction universe as the truth, unfolded with
the nominal simulation, give a spread whose ratio to that of the covariance’s construction at the
nominal truth lies within the gate’s window, 0.9–1.1, in all 16 cells: under this declared swap the
universes’ second-order departures at ±1σ are below the window, as expected, since closure fixes the
ratio at one to first order. This concerns the test; it neither validates nor undermines the reported
interaction block, and neither finding shows which spread the reported intervals should carry. In cells
R00 and R05 the interaction uncertainties carry only 0.57 and 0.36 of the squared ratio’s departure
from one, so this account does not explain the gate’s departure there, and this account says nothing
about the parts carried by the flux and detector uncertainties. A later pre-check of those two groups

at the nominal truth found that, as for the interaction group, the pseudo-experiments reproduce,
within the gate’s window in every resolved cell, the block that the same weight factors give on the
simulation side; within the detector group, the two sides give blocks outside that window for the
GEANT neutron band in cells R00, R02, R08, R09, R10 and R11 and for the GEANT proton band
in cells R01, R05, R07 and R08, with no cause established. The coverage of the result’s intervals has
not been validated. A staged coverage test at the two tunes, with the covariance rebuilt by the same
procedure at each tune, ended without a coverage result: its first stage stopped under a fail-closed
rule declared in advance. That rule stops the stage if any declared draw makes a cell of the drawn
natures’ composite denominator non-positive; 892 of the 900 declared draws did so, with at most 62
such cells in a draw. The stop concerns the declared composition of the natures, not the estimator’s
coverage, and says nothing about whether the intervals cover, in either direction.

10.6

Dependence on the unfolding algorithm

A comparison declared before it ran puts the result beside a binned D’Agostini iterative Bayesian
unfolding (IBU) of the same data, with the same simulation, the same background template and the
same weight-only systematic universes as configuration (e), at 5 iterations, integrated to the same
−1
sixteen cells. It forms T = ∆T C∆
∆ from the difference ∆ of the two results and its covariance
C∆ , which holds the statistical, universe and normalization blocks of the difference (not the model,
lateral, Cresp or run-period blocks), and compares T with bars calibrated on its own simulated null
of exact agreement. The two results differ: T = 194.4, against calibrated bars of 28.12 (0.95) and
39.04 (0.997). The reported cells therefore depend on the unfolding algorithm at the level of T . This
dependence is a stated limitation of the result: no uncertainty block is added for it, and the reported
cells and covariance are unchanged. A reading of differ means that a difference is detectable at this
noise; it does not show that either method is correct.
Descriptively, at a NuWro closure the IBU’s median absolute residual is 2.98 times that of
configuration (e), and at the simulation’s own truth, a trivial closure, the IBU’s residual vanishes;
neither is a coverage statement for either estimator. The IBU uncertainty is a median 1.37 times that
of configuration (e) over the sixteen cells (0.77–4.05); configuration (e)’s statistical block includes its
estimator’s own noise, refit per replica, and the IBU has none.

10.7

Comparison with generator predictions

Table 20 compares the result with five generator predictions. Each gives the χ2 against the reported
covariance plus Vg , Ctotal + Cresp + CPL,5D + Vg , where Vg is the prediction’s finite-sample (diagonal)
covariance, and the shape-only χ2 , with the normalization free, and both again without CPL,5D
(§10.4). No diagonal-only χ2 is formed. GiBUU’s sample has no events above Eν = 20 GeV, so it
is compared on the 8 cells below p∥ = 4.5 GeV/c only, and no value is formed on the other 8. In
the GENIE CV and NuWro samples, at least 11.71% and 12.59%, respectively, of the cross section
above p∥ = 4.5 GeV/c comes from Eν > 20 GeV. Between p∥ = 1.5 and 4.5 GeV/c, they are 0.83%
and 0.77%. These GENIE CV and NuWro fractions, not GiBUU values, are what a sample stopping
at Eν = 20 GeV would miss. All five predictions are in the analysis definitions of W and of the
muon angle, and NuWro and GiBUU, generated on carbon, are corrected to CH by adding each
generator’s own free-proton prediction with weight 1/13 per nucleon (§10.8). MINERvA Tune v1 is
the simulation’s prior, not an independent prediction. Because Cmodel holds the GENIE + MEC,
NuWro and GiBUU shifts, those comparisons each use a covariance built in part from the compared
generator, and the GENIE CV comparison does so through the reference of the ratios.
Table 21 gives each cell’s pull and (g − f )/f .

The fake-data bias exceeds the stated thresholds at the GiBUU hybrid truth under the reported
covariance, and also at the GiBUU Eavail shape without CPL,5D (§10.5), so the GiBUU comparison
carries that limitation.
The sensitivity of the reported χ2 to the correlations is probed by shrinking one group’s correlations
toward the diagonal by a fraction α of the way, with its variances kept. These are descriptive
sensitivities, not alternative results. Shrinking only the flux correlations by 10 % lowers the χ2 of the
16-cell predictions by 23–38 %. The same change to the detector or lateral correlations lowers it by at
most 18 %. With the correlations of the interaction, flux, detector and lateral groups removed, every
16-cell prediction keeps χ2 ≥ 41 for 16 degrees of freedom. Table 22 gives each group’s sensitivity,
GiBUU’s eight-cell values included. The flux correlations are themselves estimated from the 100
PPFX flux universes (§10.3). Rebuilding the flux block from 2000 bootstrap replicates of those
universes, with its variances kept, gives 16–84 % percentile bands of the total χ2 : GENIE 2.12.10
CV 104–110 around 107, GENIE 2.12.10 + Valencia MEC 222–235 around 229, NuWro 21.09 (CH)
126–130 around 128, MINERvA Tune v1 253–272 around 262, and GiBUU 2019 (CH), on its 8 cells,
47–48 around 48. In each, the bootstrap median lies above the reported value, by 0.26, 0.32, 0.20,
1.00 and 0.07 in the same order. These bands measure the estimation scatter of the reported χ2
given these universes, not how much it would change if the flux correlations were wrong, which the
shrinking above probes; they are not intervals for the χ2 under the true correlations.

10.8

The predictions’ target and definitions

The predictions of Table 20 differ from the generators’ own products in two ways. First, NuWro and
1
GiBUU, generated on carbon, are corrected to CH per fine cell as xCH = 12
13 xC + 13 xH , with xH the
generator’s own free-proton product, as in §11.9. Second, all four use the analysis definitions of W
and of the muon angle: W is computed with the struck nucleon’s mass, or an average nucleon mass
where the generator records no single struck nucleon, and the muon angle is measured from the
beam axis, each event’s neutrino direction being drawn from the analysis simulation’s distribution in
its Eν bin. The prediction is the mean over five such draws. With the generators’ own definitions
restored, every sample reproduces its committed product bit for bit. The change of definitions moves
no cell of any of the four by more than 0.063 of the cell’s reported σ, and the standard deviation
over the five draws is at most 0.052 σ. MINERvA Tune v1 is already in the analysis definitions and
is unchanged.
Table 24 gives the carbon values and the sensitivities. On carbon, in the analysis definitions,
NuWro gives 129.98 (shape-only 112.93) and GiBUU 45.49 (24.94). The correction lowers NuWro’s
total χ2 and raises GiBUU’s by 2.3, while GiBUU’s shape-only χ2 falls. On the joint cells of §11.9
both corrections move the prediction toward the data; in the sixteen-cell comparison GiBUU’s total
χ2 , on its eight cells, rises, because the CH prediction is paired with Cmodel ’s NuWro and GiBUU
shape ratios from carbon (below). With the reported Cmodel , that rise also depends on the hydrogen
sample: with GENIE CV’s hydrogen in place of GiBUU’s own, in the generators’ own definitions,
GiBUU’s total is 44.19, below its carbon value of 45.25. GENIE’s hydrogen sample is thin, so that
spread is largely its own Monte Carlo noise, and the GENIE-hydrogen rows are sensitivities, never
the comparison. GiBUU’s CH comparison carries the label of §11.9: the sampling-closure check failed
on its hydrogen sample, a bias check of that check’s premise, added post hoc, passed at ≤ 0.1 σc ,
and deep-inelastic events, 46 % of its hydrogen sample, do not conserve energy.
A native CH generation of NuWro, with only the target changed in the P
production card, was
compared cell by cell with the combination under a rule fixed in advance:
z 2 < 32.0 over the

The Cmodel components of the reported covariance are built from the generators’ samples in
their own definitions, with NuWro and GiBUU on carbon, not from the predictions of Table 20.
Table 25 measures the effect of that target. There, NuWro’s and GiBUU’s shape ratios in Cmodel are

the rise described above comes from pairing the CH prediction with the carbon shape ratios. For the
scale of Cmodel itself, whose size is not calibrated, the table also gives the χ2 with Cmodel removed
and doubled. Every χ2 falls as Cmodel grows. Removing Cmodel raises GiBUU’s total most, to 166.83
from 47.78. With the CH shape ratios, the reported per-cell σ falls in 15 of the 16 cells and rises in

in R12, where it is small: from 0.0051 to 0.0075 (+47.8 %). This measures Cmodel ’s dependence
on the target only: its definitions stay the generators’ own, its coverage is still not validated, and
GiBUU’s CH shape ratio carries the hydrogen label of §11.9. The changes are those of these data;
their statistical uncertainty, from the data entering the unfolds that build Cmodel , is not measured.

Calibrated joint tests of generator predictions

This section tests five generator predictions jointly in the five observables (pT , p∥ , Eavail , q3 , W ), each
in total and in shape. Each test evaluates a hybrid null hypothesis about one prediction (§11.1)
against a null distribution obtained by simulating the full analysis chain. The tests are hypothesis
tests, not a measured cross section, and they add no reportable uncertainty. They neither use nor
validate the five-axis covariance of the five-dimensional central value unfolded with purity-based
background subtraction (configuration (a), §9.6). The section gives the design, the six conditions
of the declared model (§11.4), the decisions (§11.5; lost pseudo-experiments: §11.6) and the later
sensitivity studies. Reference [52] gives the specification and the decisions, and each later study’s
own reference is cited where the study is described.
Two properties must be distinguished. The coverage of unfolded intervals is the rate at which
they contain the true cross section. The calibration of a test is the rate at which it rejects a
true hypothesis. Calibration can be obtained without validated intervals by simulating the whole
analysis chain at the hypothesis, so that the estimator’s bias enters the simulated null distribution.
A calibration obtained in this way is valid to the extent that the simulated chain reproduces the
analysis of the data. Unfolded results can be compared with any prediction at truth level without
simulating each generator through the detector. For hypothesis tests the advantage is smaller. A
test at reconstruction level would avoid the regularization bias, but it would still need the same
response model. Each external prediction would have to be folded through the analysis simulation’s
response, which is the hybrid construction of §11.1, with the same composition below the fine grid.
The tests of this section instead calibrate the unfolded statistic in simulation, so that the bias enters
the null distribution rather than the decision. We have not compared their power with that of a
reconstruction-level test.

11.1

Hypotheses, grid and statistics

For each of five predictions G (the MINERvA Tune v1, GENIE 2.12.10 without and with MEC,
NuWro 21.09 and GiBUU 2019; §8.5), the null H0 (G) is that the true cross section equals G on
every cell of a fine five-dimensional grid. Below that grid, the null truth has the MINERvA Tune v1
simulation’s event composition, varied in each pseudo-experiment by the drawn interaction-model
universes. Every fine-cell integral is held fixed in that variation. The background is the Tune v1
simulation’s, with its uncertainty bands. This hybrid null is the hypothesis the calibration simulates.
For the Tune v1, which is the analysis simulation itself, no external prediction is combined with the
Tune v1 composition. Three requirements are distinct. The hybrid null is defined exactly on the
finite grid, and no convergence is needed to define it. Whether it represents the physical generator
prediction depends on condition (i) (§11.4). Whether the calibration reproduces the analysis of the
data is calibration fidelity (conditions (ii)–(iv)). Each prediction is tested in total (rate and shape
together) and in shape alone: 10 tests. The fine grid is the analysis binning of §3.5, 14 × 16 × 7 × 7 × 6
bins in (pT , p∥ , Eavail , q3 , W ); the test cells below are its integrals.
The tests are evaluated on a coarse 35 grid in (pT , p∥ , Eavail , q3 , W ) with edges pT ∈
[0, 0.55, 0.85, 4.5] GeV/c, p∥ ∈ [1.5, 3.5, 6, 60] GeV/c, Eavail ∈ [0, 0.4, 1.5, 100] GeV, q3 ∈
[0, 1.2, 2, 100] GeV and W ∈ [0, 1.4, 2.2, 100] GeV. Each inner edge is the fine-grid edge
nearest a tertile of the weighted simulated truth distribution of the signal sample in that coordinate,
so the cells keep physical bin widths. That distribution is taken from the development simulation.
The tests are restricted to the 109 supported cells (J). For GiBUU, whose sample lacks high-energy
neutrinos, the domain is the 72 cells with p∥ < 6 GeV/c. The grid specifies a finite hybrid hypothesis
and a reporting statistic. It does not turn OmniFold’s event-level training into binned unfolding.

Two statistics are formed from the unfolded cell values F and the prediction µG . The total
(rate-and-shape) statistic is Ttot = r⊤ Σ−1 r with r = F − µG . The shape statistic Tsh has the same
form on the normalized cell fractions, with the normalization Jacobian applied to Σ and one cell
dropped. The metric Σ = V + diag Var(µG ) adds the prediction’s own finite-sample variance to V .
Here V is a Ledoit–Wolf-shrunk covariance [53] of 194 pilot pseudo-experiments at the Tune v1 null.
The metric defines how hypotheses are compared, and so it affects power. The null calibration below,
not the metric, sets each test’s size.
The prediction variance is diagonal because each simulated event of a prediction falls in one cell.
The pilot’s seeds are disjoint from those of the calibration, but it reuses the same simulated events
and omitted the MINOS-efficiency band. The diagonal of V matches the calibration’s null variances
(median ratio 1.00). The shrinkage, of intensity 0.015, floors about 80 of the 109 nearly degenerate
correlation directions. At the Tune v1 null the statistic therefore has a whitened trace of about 47
rather than 109. Since V was fixed before the calibration, this affects power only (§11.5). The power
at other shrinkage intensities has not been measured.

11.2

Calibration within the implemented simulation model

The null distribution of each statistic is obtained from pseudo-experiments that run the full analysis
chain at the null truth, using the joint-test estimator (§9.3). That estimator is deterministic
LightGBM OmniFold with five iterations and the negative-weight background treatment, whose
refinement classifier has 400 trees and 31 leaves. Each pseudo-experiment
• draws Poisson pseudo-data from one half of the simulation together with an independent
background source, refits the background-refinement classifier, and unfolds with the estimator
trained on the other half;
• draws one of 100 flux universes and every one of the 34 interaction-model bands at one of its
universes;
• draws the weight-based detector bands (MINOS muon-matching efficiency and the GEANT4
neutron, pion and proton interaction reweights) and the five muon and beam-angle lateral
bands through a linear surrogate;
• draws a 1.4 % normalization, the data’s rounding noise (taken from 20 real-data re-unfolds
with rounding-level input changes), and the prediction’s finite-sample residual.
The data are unfolded once, with the full simulation. The Monte Carlo p value of a test is
(k + 1)/(B + 1), where k of B null pseudo-experiments reach the observed statistic. This p value
is never zero. The p value on which each decision rests, the claim p value, is the largest over the
declared claim variants, which shift the null. The variants move it by an upper bound on the
simulation-size difference between the pseudo-experiments and the data unfold (condition (iv) below),
and by ±2 δfine (condition (i)). The external nulls are strongly non-central, with non-centralities λ
of 318–6344 in these units. They are non-central because the calibration reproduces the estimator’s
pull toward its prior (§9.3). Each observed statistic is compared with that displaced distribution
(Fig. 31).
Generative model of one pseudo-experiment. Each calibration pseudo-experiment splits the
simulation at random into two halves (Table 27) and refits the background refinement. Its truth
equals G on every fine cell that the source half populates. Cells it leaves empty lower a coarse cell
by at most 0.2 %, or about 4 % of the null standard deviation (the per-experiment spread is below
1.5 % of it). On the signal, each interaction-model band is renormalized within each fine truth cell,

so it moves only the composition within the cell, and with it the efficiency and the migrations. The
flux universe and the band universes are drawn independently, and their P
weight ratios multiply.
The muon-energy, muon-resolution, and beam-angle lateral bands enter as b zb ∆b , with ∆b from
endpoint unfoldings of the data. The detector weight bands include the GEANT4 hadron-interaction
reweights. Because the ∆b come from the observed data, the null distributions are conditional on
these data-derived inputs, which would differ in a repeated experiment. That conditioning has not
been validated.

Multiplicity, stopping rule and design

The ten tests are carried out with the Holm procedure [54] at familywise level α = 0.05. A determinacy
requirement also applies: a step rejects only when the 95% Clopper–Pearson interval [55] of k/B
lies entirely below its Holm threshold. Calibration proceeded in batches of 200 pseudo-experiments
per null, to at most 1999, under a sequential rule that stopped a null once both of its decisions
were determined. A null stopped when a 99.5% Clopper–Pearson interval of k/B lay entirely on
one side of each of its thresholds and met a fixed precision requirement. The decision rule uses the

upper end of the two-sided 95% interval, a one-sided 97.5% upper bound. Two assumptions are
made: (A1) each true null’s calibrated tail probability is uniformly distributed or conservative, and
(A2) the pseudo-experiments’ exceedances are independent given the data. Under A1 and A2, the
probability that this upper end falls below the threshold, at any number of pseudo-experiments up
to 1999, is below the threshold. This holds whatever rule determines when sampling stops, including
the budget extensions. The decision rule therefore controls the familywise error rate of the ten
decisions at 5% under these assumptions. That rate is the probability of falsely rejecting at least
one true hypothesis among them. The error control applies to the decisions, not separately to the
point estimates (k + 1)/(B + 1). Under these assumptions, the bound FWER ≤ 0.8902 α < 0.9 α is
derived in §B.5. A2 requires the lost pseudo-experiments to have been lost independently of their
outcomes, which has not been shown. When rerun, however, none of them reaches the observed
statistic (§11.6). The calibrated size is averaged over the nuisance priors: at a fixed true value of the
nuisance parameters it can exceed α.
The joint-test estimator (§11.2) was chosen before the design was fixed. The hypotheses, statistics,
variants, multiplicity rule, stopping rule and every input were fixed before any production pseudoexperiment or observed statistic, with two data-informed elements. First, the design’s reviewers had
seen descriptive per-cell residuals of the data before it was fixed. Second, a bounded extension of the
production budget was decided during production, while the calibration’s monitoring output showed
observed claim p values, so that the sequential rule, rather than the budget, could set the stop. No
statistic, variant, threshold or stopping rule was changed, and the familywise bound above holds
whatever rule determined when sampling stopped. An independent recomputation from the stored
products, with separately written code, reproduces every p value and decision of the production
evaluation. It verifies the calculation, not the adequacy of the calibration. The calibration is valid for
the simulated null to the extent that the pseudo-experiment chain reproduces the analysis applied to
the data. No rejection rate has been measured for null pseudo-data processed like the data, which
would require an independent simulation sample.
Three studies followed the decisions: the scan of the shift multiplier (§11.5), the rerun of the lost
pseudo-experiments (§11.6), and the matched coarse-projection comparison (§11.7). They are for
reporting only, outside the decision rule. Each was specified before it ran; the projection comparison
was specified after the five-dimensional outcomes were known.

11.4

Conditions

Each decision holds under six conditions of the declared model. They are restated here in the terms
of this note, numbered (i)–(vi) as in the test specification, and fall into three kinds.
Numerical convergence. Condition (i) concerns the null truth below the fine grid. There it has
the Tune v1 composition of the hypothesis definition (§11.1), varied by the drawn interaction-model
universes, with every fine-cell integral held fixed at that of G, including the finite-sample fluctuations
of G’s simulation. The fine-grid convergence check has not shown convergence. In the L2 norm of the
per-cell relative changes, the last refinement, from a twofold-merged grid to the fine grid, is 0.50–0.86
of the whole change from the coarse test grid to the fine grid. In that norm the successive refinement
changes are therefore not shown to shrink, and the residual below the fine grid is unmeasured and
potentially material. The design assumes that each twofold refinement of the grid multiplies the
refinement change, measured in a proxy of the test metric, by a factor of 0.45–0.76. This factor
comes from the review of the design, and its derivation is not given. If the change kept shrinking
geometrically at that rate, the residual would be about one to three times the last refinement change,
δfine . Here δfine is the change of the noise-free unfolded result when the null truth is refined from the

twofold-merged grid to the fine grid. That estimate sets the claim variants at ±2 δfine , the central
extrapolated estimate, and the robustness variants, for reporting only, at ±3 δfine . The extrapolation
assumes geometric convergence. The measured L2 ratios also allow slower rates and larger residuals.
Measured with V fixed before any observed statistic, the claim variants move the external nulls
by 0.55–2.0 null standard deviations and the robustness variants by 1.07–3.6. For the hybrid null
as simulated, the residual plays no role. The variants carry the rejections over to G itself only if
replacing the Tune v1’s shape below the fine grid by G’s moves the noise-free unfolded result by no
more than ±2 δfine along δfine . Condition (vi) concerns the predictions’ own finite simulated samples,
which enter the metric as diag Var(µG ) and the calibration as a drawn residual term.
Calibration validity. The calibration’s validity, and what the independent recomputation verifies,
are stated in §11.3. Two known departures are bounded. Condition (iv) concerns the simulation size:
the pseudo-experiments unfold with half of the simulation and the data with all of it. The resulting
shift D, estimated from 16 pairs, is at noise level per cell, with a median |D|/f of 0.02–0.075%.
Along the null’s bias direction it is not always consistent with zero (proxy a/se of +4.7 for the
Tune v1 and −3.4 for NuWro), but it is at most 0.068 null standard deviations. That bound was
measured with V fixed before any observed statistic, and it supersedes the specification’s estimate of
about 0.05. The shift enters as the bias-aligned upper-bound variant. That shift was measured for
the mean only, in a process without noise or nuisance draws. A separate check reran the first 200
calibration pseudo-experiments of the NuWro, GENIE 2.12.10, and GENIE 2.12.10 + MEC nulls
with a quarter of the simulation. Between half and quarter simulation, it resolved no change of the
null distributions’ location, width, or tails: the medians agree within 0.074 null standard deviations,
and the widths lie within 0.975–1.010 of the half-simulation values. The only change it resolved is a
mean paired shift of 0.006–0.034 null standard deviations. Under an assumed extrapolation from
half to full simulation that is conservative by design, all three shape decisions, and so all ten, remain
rejected. The check concerns the simulation size only and is not a test of the data process. Every
pseudo-experiment resamples the same simulated events, so the null distributions are conditional
on that simulation. The decisions closest to their boundary are the shape tests of NuWro and
GENIE 2.12.10. With the Holm order held fixed, their observed statistics exceed the value at which
the decision would change by 1.11 and 1.30 null standard deviations, and by 1.01 and 0.67 with the
robustness variants at ±3 δfine . The other eight tests lie 2.7 to 203 null standard deviations from
that value, and 2.0 to 203 with the robustness variants. Crossing that value would make a test
undetermined, its Clopper–Pearson interval straddling the threshold, rather than not rejected. The
data unfold’s rounding-level nondeterminism, measured over the 20 re-unfolds, leaves every observed
p value unchanged except NuWro’s shape test (2/1752 to 3/1752).
Physical-model adequacy. Condition (ii) concerns the detector model. It is the simulation’s per
event, varied only by the drawn detector bands (MINOS muon-matching efficiency and the GEANT4
neutron, pion and proton reweights, each at ±1σ) and by the linear surrogate for the five lateral
bands. The surrogate has been checked only for the symmetry of its endpoints. The analysis’s
systematic set (App. A) has no recoil-energy-scale band, so the completeness of the hadronic response
is not established (§11.8). Condition (iii) concerns the interaction-model prior, the analysis’s set
of 34 interaction-model reweighting bands, with every band drawn in every pseudo-experiment.
Negative weights in two of them, LowQ2_1 and HighQ2_1, are clipped per universe. Measured
on the pilot, the effect of clipping them rather than keeping them is at most 0.35% of a band’s
shift in whitened norm. Condition (v) concerns the neutrino-energy range. The GiBUU sample
contains no events with Eν > 20 GeV (§8.5), and its truth outside the test domain (p∥ ≥ 6 GeV/c,

with truncated Eν ) enters through the migrations into the domain cells. No prediction contains
events with Eν > 100 GeV; only the data’s flux integral stops there, and the omission is negligible.
The use of the Tune v1 composition below the fine grid in (i) is also a modelling choice of this kind.

11.5

Decisions

Table 28 lists the ten decisions. Every test is rejected at familywise α = 0.05; nine have k = 0, and
NuWro’s shape test has k = 1. Each rejection also survives the robustness variants at ±3 δfine , the
upper end of the extrapolation in condition (i). Figure 31 shows each null’s calibrated distribution
with the observed statistic. The Tune v1 null is centred at small values, whereas the external nulls
are displaced far upward by the estimator’s pull toward its prior.

Table 28: Joint five-dimensional generator tests, in Holm order; every test is rejected. B is the
number of completed calibration pseudo-experiments at the stop set by the sequential rule (1400
submitted per null, NuWro 1800; the rest were lost to time limits and later recovered for reporting
only, §11.6), and k the number at least as extreme as the data; claim p = (k + 1)/(B + 1) is never
zero.
Sensitivity to the sub-fine-grid residual. The multiplier κ scales the last measured refinement
change δfine : κ = 1 means one such change, not one standard deviation or a confidence level. A scan
of κ along the direction δfine of the claim variants [56] (for reporting only) found, in the calibration
ensembles used for the decisions, all ten decisions to remain determinate rejections at every sampled
multiplier up to 5.25. Determinate rejection is first lost between the sampled multipliers 5.2547
and 5.2563, and between 5.1688 and 5.1703 with the recovered draws included. It is lost by the
GENIE 2.12.10 + MEC shape test, whose interval then contains its Holm threshold, and, through
the Holm step-down, by the GENIE 2.12.10 shape test. Neither is then a determinate failure to
reject. The scan probes one prescribed direction. It establishes neither convergence nor a bound on
the actual below-grid residual (condition (i), §11.4).
Power. Because every test rejects, power serves here only as context: the test specification uses
power only to interpret a failure to reject. At the Tune v1 null, two of the three declared alternatives
are detected in shape with power 1.0 at the 0.005 level, and the third, a MEC-like (Eavail , W )
shape, with 0.231. At the GENIE 2.12.10 null the shape powers at 0.005 are 0.739, 0.02 and 0.00.
The binomial 95% intervals are [0.98, 1] for the first two Tune v1 alternatives, [0.17, 0.30] for the
third, and [0.67, 0.80], [0.01, 0.05] and [0, 0.02] at the GENIE 2.12.10 null. These values use the
power sets as evaluated for the decisions. Each of those sets had lost some of its 200 alternative

11.6

Lost pseudo-experiments and their recovery

Computing time limits interrupted or prevented 224 of the submitted calibration pseudo-experiments
(111 interrupted, 113 never started) and 53 power pseudo-experiments. The decisions in Table 28 were
evaluated on the completed draws. A sensitivity analysis computed before the lost outcomes were
known showed that every decision could change under a realizable assignment of them. Had every
interrupted draw exceeded the observed statistic, no test would have been rejected (p 0.013–0.019).
Among completed draws, runtime is not associated with the statistic (|ρ| ≤ 0.06), which supports
but does not certify the decisions.
The lost seeds were rerun under a procedure that was fixed, and reviewed independently, before
the rerun [57]. A determinism check fixed in advance (16 of 16 completed seeds rerun bitwise
identically) shows that each recovered draw is the lost draw, so every submitted draw has been
observed. No recovered draw reaches the observed statistic in any test, claim variant or robustness
variant, so no assignment of the lost draws changes any decision. The closest margin is 4.6 units of
T below the observed value of 868.6 (GENIE 2.12.10 + MEC shape, ±3 δfine variant). The next is
49.8 (GENIE 2.12.10 shape, same variant). With complete batches the predeclared rule would have
stopped the Tune v1, GENIE 2.12.10, GENIE 2.12.10 + MEC and GiBUU nulls one batch earlier
(B = 1200), with the same decisions.
The recovered draws were evaluated in two ways: with the variant shifts of the primary evaluation
held fixed (the fixed-shift evaluation), and with the shifts recomputed on all draws (the recomputedshift evaluation). The recovery’s checks are narrower in scope than the main recomputation. Only
the fixed-shift evaluation was cross-checked independently. The recomputed-shift evaluation was
reproduced only by a standalone rerun of the same code, a consistency check rather than an
independent recomputation. The earlier stops rest on the calibration run’s own re-application of
the sequential rule and have no independent check. The decisions of Table 28 remain those of the
evaluation before recovery, at the B at which its rule stopped. The observed outcomes of the lost
draws supersede the finding of the sensitivity analysis described above.

11.7

Matched coarse projections

The same calibrated tests are restricted to matched 3 × 3 coarse projections of the same cells,
onto (pT , p∥ ) and onto (Eavail , W ). The comparison uses the same calibration ensembles, with the
recovered draws included, and the same variants and claim rule. Its p values were reproduced
independently [58]. The projected tests do not enter the Holm family and change no decision of
Table 28. The comparison and its criterion were specified after the five-dimensional outcomes were
known but before the comparison was run. Under the criterion, a test would count as evidence of
additional joint discrimination only if both of its projected claim p values were at least 0.05 in both
the fixed-shift and the recomputed-shift evaluations. No test meets it, and the values are identical in
the two evaluations. Every test has p < 0.05, without a multiplicity correction, in at least one of the
two projections. This study therefore provides no evidence of additional discrimination from the joint
five-observable information. Table 29 gives every projected claim p value. In the muon-only (pT , p∥ )
projection, GENIE 2.12.10 is not rejected (p = 0.48 in total, 0.63 in shape; a post hoc observation),
whereas its (Eavail , W ) projection is. Hadronic information therefore participates in that rejection.

This does not establish added power of the joint test over all matched projections, or accept GENIE
as true. The powers of the joint and projected tests have not been compared.

11.8

Hadronic-response condition and recoil-energy sensitivity

MINERvA’s recoil-energy analyses assign a hadronic-energy response uncertainty derived from
test-beam calorimetry [59], separate from the GEANT hadron-interaction reweights [3, 8]. In
the low-recoil measurement the resulting hadronic-energy uncertainty rises to 10 % at its highest
three-momentum transfers, 0.9 < q3 < 1.2 GeV [8]. The declared nuisance model here includes
the reweights but no calorimetric response band. Such a band would act on all five nulls together,
through the shared detector simulation and unfolding. A response mismodelling could move each
observed statistic in either direction, and a band drawn in the calibration would also broaden and
could shift each null distribution. The net effect on each p value is unmeasured. The published
comparison in muon kinematics alone, whose observables do not use the reconstructed recoil energy,
also disfavours the same generator families [3]. It provides context for a disagreement involving
muon observables, but the hypotheses tested here use a different estimator and calibration. The
GENIE 2.12.10 projection result of §11.7 shows that the information rejecting that prediction involves
the hadronic axes, on which a response band would act.
In an exploratory sensitivity study of the response [60], the data are re-unfolded with the
simulation’s reconstructed recoil energy scaled by ±4 %, and the ten tests are re-evaluated against
the null distributions used for the decisions. This scale is the level of data–simulation agreement
reported for test-beam protons, pions and electrons of 0.35–2.0 GeV/c [59]. Neutrons and highermomentum hadrons, which matter at high W , lie outside that sample. The ±4% scale is not a
validated uncertainty prescription. A re-unfold with unscaled recoil reproduces the decisions of
Table 28, with every claim p value inside the range spanned by the data’s rounding variations. At
both −4% and +4% every one of the ten rejections holds. The observed statistics move by up to
about 11 % from their recorded values. Nine tests keep k = 0. NuWro’s shape test, the closest to its
null, changes from k = 1 to k = 3 of 1751 at +4% (p = 0.0023, against a Holm threshold of 0.05).
The evaluation was reproduced independently. Table 30 gives the observed statistics.
The study measures the sensitivity of the observed statistics and decisions to one coherent scale
of reconstructed available energy and recoil energy, applied after the energy calibration at a single

magnitude. It leaves untested (a) the response of the null distributions, which were not regenerated
with the variation although they are simulated through the same detector model, so the size of this
omission is unmeasured for these strongly non-central nulls; (b) particle-species, energy-dependent
or resolution effects, and a response difference applied before calibration; and (c) larger variations.
The rejections are therefore conditional on the simulation’s hadronic response, and the response
model is not shown to be complete.

11.9

Hydrocarbon target correction for NuWro and GiBUU

NuWro 21.09 and GiBUU 2019 were generated on carbon, while the data and the other predictions are
per nucleon of CH. This study corrects the two predictions to CH and re-evaluates their tests against
the null distributions used for the decisions. These target-corrected evaluations are descriptive; the
decisions of Table 28 are unchanged.
1
Construction. Each prediction is rebuilt per fine cell as xCH = 12
13 xC + 13 xH . Here xH is the
generator’s own free-proton product, run through the unchanged conversion, flux-repair and binning
chain. Each prediction’s Monte Carlo variance is combined with the squares of the same weights.
The hydrogen fraction is fH = 5.08% for NuWro over the grid and 4.77% for GiBUU over its 72
cells. Over the reported cells, the ratio rc = µCH /µC has minimum, median, and maximum 0.923,
0.963, and 1.039 for NuWro (109 cells) and 0.923, 0.963, and 1.036 for GiBUU (72 cells). Per cell, fH
reaches 0.112 and 0.109. On these cells both corrections move the prediction toward the data; in the
sixteen-cell comparison, GiBUU’s total χ2 on its eight cells rises with the correction, because the
corrected prediction is paired with Cmodel ’s NuWro and GiBUU shape ratios from carbon (§10.8).

Re-evaluated tests. With the metric used for the decisions, NuWro’s total statistic falls by 3.83
null standard deviations and its shape statistic by 0.37. At its original Holm position (threshold 0.005)
the total test would then lie 1.11 null standard deviations below its boundary, and so be undetermined.
It remains rejected only because its larger p value moves it to the last Holm position (threshold
0.05). In the κ = 3 family, which is for reporting only, it is not rejected (p = 0.075). NuWro’s shape
test stays rejected, 0.74 null standard deviations above its decision boundary. GiBUU’s statistics fall
by 4.67 (total) and 1.33 (shape) null standard deviations. Both tests stay rejected at their original
Holm positions, including at κ = 3. Their margins shrink from 7.86 to 3.18 and from 3.58 to 2.24
null standard deviations. With each CH prediction’s own variance in the metric, no decision changes
for either generator.

Generation checks. For GiBUU, the sampling-closure check failed on the hydrogen sample. A
bias check of that check’s premise was added post hoc. Its criterion, a change of at most 0.1 σc in
every cell, was declared before the check was computed. The check met the criterion: it moved the
CH prediction by at most 5.1 × 10−4 σc . Here σc is the standard deviation of the cell’s result over
NuWro’s null distribution used for the decisions (B = 1751). The GiBUU re-evaluation is reported
under the label “B3 (sampling closure) failed on hydrogen; a post-hoc bias check of its premise passed
at ≤ 0.1 σc ”. It has a further limitation: GiBUU’s deep-inelastic events on hydrogen, 46% of its
hydrogen sample, do not conserve energy. They lose a median of 45 MeV, with a median transversemomentum imbalance of 31 MeV, while its other hydrogen events balance exactly. The cause has
not been identified. The available energy of those events may be low, and the non-conservation may
also affect GiBUU’s carbon prediction, which cannot be checked in this way. In NuWro’s hydrogen
generation, four chunks aborted and were rerun on alternate seeds. The fraction of events this
excludes is estimated as 1.8 × 10−5 in the main sample and 3.8 × 10−5 in the 50–100 GeV supplement,
or 1.9 × 10−5 and 6.2 × 10−5 including the first attempt. These estimates rest on four to six events.
The supplement’s 95% upper bound of 1.8 × 10−4 exceeds the 10−4 level set for it, so the supplement
is flagged rather than assumed negligible.

11.10

What the tests establish

The comparisons recover the generator disagreements already established by the published twodimensional measurement on the same data [3]. Their methodological contribution is calibration
within the implemented simulation model, which puts the estimator’s bias toward its prior and
the declared nuisances into each null distribution. The rejections are global assessments of the five
specified predictions under the declared model. Assigning a discrepancy to a kinematic region or
nuclear mechanism would require separate tests. The rejections apply to the generator versions listed
in §8.5, and they retain all six conditions of §11.4. Extending the inference would require a response
band drawn through the calibration and a demonstrated bound on the residual below the fine grid.

12

Extended fiducial phase-space study

This section gives a preliminary scalar central value for an extended fiducial domain, with a threeprior dependence envelope, a hidden-variable closure and a 200-pseudo-experiment containment
diagnostic for the extension. The signal remains νµ CC in the tracker interaction fiducial volume. The
truth-muon kinematic cuts are removed, but the reconstructed MINOS-based selection is unchanged,
so the domain is an extended fiducial one, not unrestricted neutrino phase space. The result consists
only of central values and these validation tests, without a quoted covariance.

12.1

Acceptance and the extended domain

The published cuts (θµ < 20◦ , 1.5 < p∥ < 60 GeV/c, pT < 4.5 GeV/c) exclude a third of the signal.
Of the weighted true νµ CC rate in the tracker fiducial volume, 66.4 % lies inside the cuts, 22.3 % at
p∥ < 1.5 GeV/c, 11.3 % at θµ > 20◦ , and the high-pT /p∥ tails are negligible (Fig. 32). OmniFold’s
treatment of events that fail reconstruction already corrects for acceptance, so removing the truthlevel kinematic cuts is a change of configuration only: the truth denominator grows from 32.8 million
to 49 million events. In regions without acceptance the unfolding extrapolates through the step-1
regressor for missed events; the validation tests below quantify the resulting prior dependence.

Figure 32: Extended-fiducial acceptance for all twelve data-taking periods: (a) weighted truth rate
(log10 ), (b) reconstruction efficiency, and (c) the low-acceptance region, ϵ < 2 %, on the extended
(pT , p∥ ) grid (bin number; the overflow bins are too wide for a linear axis). Cyan lines mark the
published boundary. Efficiency is ∼ 0.65 and flat through the published region, degrades smoothly
across the θ > 20◦ wedge, and falls to zero in the p∥ < 1.5 GeV/c strip and the high-pT corner. The
low-acceptance region carries 27.7 % of the total truth rate.

12.2

Validation tests

Four tests characterize the extension (Fig. 33):
1. Published-region check. The control unfolds the standard 2D input on the published
binning, with settings identical to the extended-domain unfolding’s. In the published sub-block,
the extended-domain unfolding reproduces it with integral ratio 0.9994, median cell ratio
1.0013 and median absolute relative deviation from the control 0.57 % over all 185 reported
cells. The control’s total, 3.073 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon, agrees at the printed precision with the
purity-subtracted 2D totals (exact-split and LightGBM). The quoted 2D result, which uses
refined negative-weight subtraction, is 3.069 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon, 0.14 % lower, so the control
does not reproduce it.
2. Closure. Extended-grid self-closure using simulated reconstruction as pseudo-data returns
recovered/truth = 1.0000 in every cell, including the extension, with no event-accounting or
normalization bias from the enlarged denominator. In hidden-variable closure, a Gaussian
truth bump is injected in true Eavail , unseen by the (pT , p∥ ) unfolding. The per-cell absolute
relative deviation of the recovered cross section from the bump-reweighted (pT , p∥ ) truth has
a median of 0.17 % (90th percentile 0.65 %) in the published cells and a median of 0.77 %
(maximum 4.05 %) in the extension, both well inside the three-prior envelope.
3. Three-prior envelope. The total cross section varies by ±1.5 % across three priors (MINERvA
Tune v1, bare GENIE and NuWro-shaped). The NuWro-shaped prior reweights the truth
(pT , p∥ ) shape to two million raw NuWro events without phase-space cuts. The sample’s
mis-sampled flux (§7.6) suppresses its high-p∥ shape, and this prior was not rebuilt after the
flux repair. The per-cell half-spread of the three results, divided by their mean, has a median
of 2.9 % in the published region and 7.9 % (90th percentile 62 %) in the extension, concentrated
in zero-acceptance cells (Fig. 34).
4. Containment diagnostic. With a fixed estimator seed and one disjoint 100/100 split into
calibration and evaluation sets, widths learned from the calibration set contain the fixed truth
in 68.67 % of evaluation (bin, pseudo-experiment) pairs, compared with the 68.27 % Gaussian

nominal. Bin correlations and estimated widths preclude a global binomial error or a calibrated
test, and the closure pseudo-experiments fluctuate statistically only, so a full uncertainty band
is not tested. The statistical-band, pull and regional fractions are in App. B.3; none is used to
certify a covariance.
