Figure 34: Three-prior dependence envelope (per-cell half-spread/mean, in percent). Prior dependence
is at the few-percent level wherever the detector has acceptance and reaches tens of percent only in
low-acceptance cells, the quantitative basis for the two-tier reporting.

12.3

Central value and two-tier reporting

The preliminary extended-fiducial CC-inclusive central value on the tracker fiducial volume is
σext = 4.502 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon (central value),

compared with 3.073 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon for the control unfolding in the restricted phase space.
The extended-fiducial central value was produced with the binned purity correction, not with refined
negative-weight subtraction, the treatment designated for the extended-fiducial analysis (App. C).
We adopt two-tier reporting. Tier 1 comprises the acceptance-supported cells (ϵ ≳ 2%): the
published region and the measurable part of the extension, which carries ∼ 6 % of the rate, chiefly
in the θ > 20◦ wedge and the p∥ ∈ [0.75, 1.5] GeV/c edge. Tier 2, prior-extrapolated, comprises the
low-acceptance cells (∼ 28 % of the rate) and is flagged separately with an explicit additional threeprior envelope band. Both tiers enter the sum; only tier 1 is interpreted as an acceptance-supported
measurement. No uncertainty magnitude is assigned beyond the three-prior envelope, because no
extended-domain covariance has been constructed with re-selected lateral bands under the procedure
of §4.5.

13

Summary and outlook

Summary. Beyond the results stated with their scopes in the executive summary (p. 1), the body
supports two further conclusions.
• The refined negative-weight subtraction, which the sixteen-cell result uses (§10), largely removes
the bias that purity-based background subtraction leaves in the highest-W cells (§9.2). In a
two-dimensional closure test with backgrounds, the purity and refined estimates differ by more
than one statistical standard deviation in 14 of the 205 cells. In those cells the purity estimate
lies up to 5.9 σstat from the truth, and the refined one within 0.44 σstat . On this basis, the
two-dimensional result uses the refined estimator (App. C).
• The joint tests’ design was fixed before any production pseudo-experiment or observed statistic,
with two data-informed elements (§11.3). The tests recover established discrepancies and show
no evidence of discrimination beyond matched coarse projections (§11).
Principal limitations. The executive summary lists the principal limitations (p. 2), each with
the section that states it. The quoted two-dimensional statistical block has had no coverage test
(§4.2, §5.2).
Outlook. The scalar extended-fiducial study (§12) yields a preliminary central value σext =
4.502 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon, against 3.073 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon for its control unfolding in the
restricted phase space. New acceptance-supported cells contribute about 6 % of the rate; separately
labeled prior-dominated extrapolation contributes about 28 %. Further refinements are a fifth
external generator, NEUT, and a finer 44-cell comparison with the Ascencio et al. measurement
(the maximal-common-grid comparison is in §7.8). A flux and muon-energy-scale posterior derived
with MAT-MINERvA, independently of the published analysis, would determine the correlation ρ
between the leading flux mode and the muon-energy-scale band (§4) entirely within this analysis.
Reusing the unbinned result. The public ATLAS charged-particle phase-space release [61]
exemplifies unbinned distribution. The emerging omnifold_publication proposal [62] emphasizes
weight-family metadata, normalization, row alignment, and checksums. A MINERvA release
should likewise specify event identity, estimator and uncertainty-family definitions, units, support,
normalization, and a reproducible projection example. App. G specifies this release for our analysis,
including which of its objects already have fixed identities and which are still to be produced. A
local release candidate exists, with checksums and a verifier that recomputes and checks its results;

no release has been tagged or deposited. Rebuilding this note’s PDFs assembles documents
and reproduces no scientific result (App. G). New projections require their own applicability checks;
nominal weights alone cannot certify arbitrary downstream inference (App. B.4). The release design
and its public example have no bearing on the choice of the five-axis covariance of the configuration-(a)
central value (§9.6).

Guide to the appendices
The appendices fall into three groups. The first gives methods and context for the body: statistical
methods and the 2D comparison (App. A) and those for the multidimensional results and inference
(App. B); unbinned background subtraction (App. C); the protocol for a detector-response-mismatch
closure, which rescales the reconstructed available energy of the pseudo-data while OmniFold keeps
its nominal response (App. D); and the survey of MINERvA measurement dimensionality (App. E).
The second, on future work, covers point-cloud (PET) representations (App. F). The third describes
the result package and what it reproduces (App. G) and the code layout (App. H); it is reference
material and is not needed to follow the physics results. The provenance of the records the note
cites, the fixed identities and audit record of the five-axis covariance of the configuration-(a) central
value, the test-suite discipline, and superseded values and withdrawn results are kept in the analysis
repository’s records document, Ref. [37]; each citation names the entry’s number or heading.
Labels used in the repository. The body describes each object in words: the learner backends
(§3.2), the background-subtraction modes (§3.1, App. C), the two centerings of the five-axis covariance
(§9.6), the re-selected universes (§4.7) and the two universe sweeps (§4.4). Where a repository label is

needed to find the object, it is given once in parentheses at first use. The learner backends are labelled
lgbm, exact and hist: LightGBM (the quoted central values and their uncertainty ensembles; also the
joint-test estimator), the scikit-learn exact-split estimator (the exact-split cross-check) and the scikitlearn histogram estimator (§3.2). Products described as flux-corrected are those repaired for an audit
finding that the N -dimensional code divided every flux universe by the central-value flux (App. C).
The calls behind Eq. (10), §A.2.3 and §A.1 (default cutoff) are np.cov(Xrep, rowvar=False,
ddof=1), np.random.default_rng().poisson(1.0) and np.linalg.pinv; the earlier MAT check
without the normalization band (§A.2.6) used the option --add-norm 0.

A

Statistical methods and the 2D comparison

This appendix gives the statistical methods of this note’s results, centered on the 2D comparison:
covariance inversion; the training-seed, bootstrap and universe estimators, with the Poisson limit
and the ±1σ pair covariance; their MAT-conformant construction and combination; the χ2 , pull and
shape-only conventions; the rank and conditioning of the covariances; the anatomy of the tension in
the 2D comparison; the closure procedures; and a summary of the 2D comparison numbers. It covers
analysis-specific conventions, not general statistics. The χ2 convention used throughout is Eq. (17) in
§A.3.2. The N-D declarations, scalar-5D reductions, extended-fiducial fractions and unfolded-event
inference are in App. B; the 2D fixed-truth coverage test is in §5.2 and the same-ensemble pull
diagnostic in §5.4.

A.1

Numerical conventions: inverting a covariance (Cholesky, ridge jitter,
pseudo-inverse)

Cholesky and ridge jitter. C is positive-definite (PD) iff its Cholesky factorization C = LL⊤
exists with lower-triangular L; then Eq. (17) is well-posed. Numerical noise that leaves a PD matrix
barely non-PD is repaired with an isotropic ridge ε I, set to

Rank deficiency at N ≤ n. A sample covariance from N ≤ n replicas in n bins has rank at most
N − 1. The 300-replica bootstrap (N = 300 > 205) is full-rank. The rank of the systematic block is
limited at any universe count (§A.4).
Pseudo-inverse. For the χ2 against the paper covariance, the comparison replaces C−1 with the

A.2.2

Poisson bootstrap

A coherent bootstrap carries each data factor through step-1 classifier training and final cross-section
construction. Each MC factor is shared by the reco-tree and truth-tree copies, preserving migration
consistency. The three sub-RNGs (data, signal MC, background MC; Table 31) make the sources
uncorrelated by construction, and pinning the GBDT seed isolates the bootstrap from the ML noise
of §A.2.1. A Poisson(1) factor can be zero, so some events are dropped from each replica, mimicking
a resample with replacement; Poisson(1) is the large-N limit of the multinomial counts (§A.2.3).
Each replica’s 14 × 16 histogram becomes a 205-vector X(k) of reported bins, row-major over (pT , p∥ ),
with unbiased sample covariance

A.2.3

Multinomial → independent Poisson bootstrap limit

Drawing N events with replacement from an
P N -event sample gives counts (n1 , . . . , nN ) ∼
Multinomial(N, p) with uniform pi = 1/N and i ni = N . Each marginal ni ∼ Binomial(N, 1/N )
has mean 1, variance (N − 1)/N → 1, and converges
P to Poisson(1) as N grows (many trials, small
success probability, fixed expected count). The
ni = N constraint induces O(1/N ) correlations
that vanish at large N . The multinomial is an independent Poisson process conditioned on N
counts; removing that constraint gives independent Poisson(1) variables (Poissonization). One
Poisson(1)
factor is drawn per event. For N ≳ 106 , our regime, the relative total-count error is
√
O(1/ N ), far smaller than the per-bin Poisson variance.
A.2.4

Universes

The universe sweep used by the quoted budget (the July sweep) has 187 universes across 44 bands,
dominated by the 100-universe PPFX flux band (hadron-production sampling). The quoted band
sum drops one band (Rvp1pi) and holds 43 bands plus the normalization. Almost all other bands
are ±1σ pairs (Nu = 2). They include the MINOS and lateral MINERvA muon energy scales, the
MINOS muon-match efficiency, the GENIE parameters MaRES, MvRES and MaCCQE, and the lateral
GEANT FSI responses for neutrons, pions and protons. Where selection migration matters, the five
kinematic bands (the two beam-angle components, the muon resolution and the two muon energy
scales) require re-selected event loops, run with one universe active at a time (weight-only bands:
§4.7). The quoted universe block takes these five bands from re-unfolds of re-selected universes.

Eqs. (5) and (6)).
A.2.6

How MINERvA builds its published covariance

MAT’s C++ framework defines the covariance construction that the published paper leaves unspecified. It is the mean-centered 1/N per-band sample covariance and element-wise band sum used
here.
Ruterbories 2021, Section V. The paper evaluates three uncertainty categories (flux, detector
response and the neutrino-interaction model) by “re-extracting the cross section using modified
simulations”, i.e. the universe method, and gives no covariance formula. It reports a flux uncertainty below 4% across most of phase space as a result of the ν-e-scattering constraint (Valencia,
per-particle GEANT inelastic variations; and a 1% muon energy scale constrained by the low-recoil
fit of Bashyal (JINST 16 P08068). It states that “the fit causes the flux and muon energy scale
uncertainties to be correlated and that correlation is propagated to the final result”; this is the only
correlation between bands that the paper specifies. The only covariance in its equations is V in the
model-test χ2 (Eq. 1, “the measurement covariance matrix”), with 205 degrees of freedom (Table I);
the paper also gives a log-normal χ2 variant, motivated by the highly correlated flux normalization
(Peelle’s Pertinent Puzzle). The paper itself gives no per-band covariance construction, no ±1σ pair
formula and no band-independence or block-sum statement. MAT’s code supplies the per-band
construction and the independent band sum, but no separate pair formula (below). Nor does it state
the rank, condition number and regularization of V , or any bootstrap or coverage validation (it uses
D’Agostini iterative unfolding).
MAT per band and in total. MnvVertErrorBand::CalcCovMx implements Eq. (11), referenced
to the universe mean ⟨x⟩u rather than XCV , normalized by biased 1/N rather than Bessel’s 1/(N − 1)
(a 1% offset for PPFX, N = 100), with no special case for N = 2. The conventional CV-centered pair

formula (“MINERvA-101 ±1σ pair”), 12 (∆+ ∆⊤+ + ∆− ∆⊤− ), agrees with it only for CV-symmetric
shifts. The quoted universe block, filled into a PlotUtils::MnvH1D with MAT’s universe weights on
Flux, agrees with MnvH1D::GetTotalErrorMatrix to 1.3 × 10−16 of its largest element; the quoted
Flux band carries MAT’s ν–e and inverse-muon-decay constraint weights. An earlier check compared
MnvH1D::GetTotalErrorMatrix with our band sum without the normalization band, over 44 bands
/ 187√universes, and gave a maximum difference of 5.5 × 10−17 of the largest element (machine ε)
and TrC matching to seven digits. That check could not detect that the Flux band omitted those
constraint weights, because it filled the MnvH1D without MAT’s per-universe weights (SetUnivWgts),
so both sides were unweighted; it also ran on a sweep from before the flux fix, not on a quoted
product.
MnvH1D::GetTotalErrorMatrix sums bands and the statistical matrix element-wise, assuming
band independence. Cross-band correlations exist only if encoded in a combined band, such as the
Bashyal joint fit handled outside this loop. MAT applies no ridge, shrinkage, SVD truncation or
rank-deficiency handling to the published total covariance. The statistical block makes V full-rank;
its systematic-only rank is 120, below the 138 of our universe block (eigenvalues above 10−12 λmax ;
§A.4).
Upstream references that set each band’s ±1σ amplitude.
• Flux / PPFX (100 universes), Aliaga, PRD 94 092005 (2016), arXiv:1607.00704;
• ν-e flux constraint (< 4%), Valencia, PRD 100 092001 (2019), arXiv:1906.00111;
• muon-E scale ↔ flux fit, Bashyal, JINST 16 (2021) P08068;
• test-beam / GEANT hadron response, Aliaga, NIM A 789 28 (2015), arXiv:1501.06431;
• GENIE reweighting parameters, Andreopoulos, NIM A 614 87 (2010), and the GENIE manual
• 1.4% normalization (detector mass), Aliaga, NIM A 743 130 (2014), arXiv:1305.5199.
A.2.7

Block-sum combination

The bootstrap varies events through distinct data, signal-MC and background-MC sub-RNGs;
universes vary model weights at a pinned GBDT seed; the training-seed block varies the GBDT
seed over five settings, each on the same 20 bootstrap replicas (at seed 42, replicas of the statistical
block); the run-period block compares separate unfolds of three run periods. The bootstrap and the
universes use the central value’s LightGBM seed (42), and the training-seed settings include it. The
block sum is
Ctot = CML + Cstat + Csyst + CPL .
(16)
The comparison adds OmniFold covariances to the paper’s TotalCovariance by this rule. CML is
the smallest block √
(Table 33); it is included in the quoted total but numerically negligible.
The combined TrC is the quadrature sum of the component square-root traces. The per-bin
median 6.414% is not the quadrature sum of the component medians; both combined entries are
evaluated on the summed covariance. The relative entries divide by the quoted central value, the
LightGBM seed-42 central value of the universe sweep; dividing the combined covariance by the
exact-split cross-check instead gives a combined median of 6.462 %. The universe, statistical and
training-seed blocks are LightGBM ensembles, the first two at the central value’s training seed and
the third over five settings that include it (§3.2). The universe and statistical blocks together give
5.982%, again evaluated on their sum rather than combined from the printed medians.
Tables 32 and 33 give the blocks of the quoted 2D covariance (§4).

A.3

Comparing to the paper

These conventions and tables support the comparison and interpretation limits in §6.2.
A.3.1

The 205-bin selection

The paper reports d2 σ/(dpT dp∥ ) on 14 pT bins × 16 p∥ bins = 224 cells (edges in minerva_paper_anc/bin_mapping.txt). Of these, 205 have xpaper > 0 in data_result_ptpl_2D_minerva_inclusive_6GeV.txt. The paper suppresses the remaining 19 through an efficiency cut; every χ2
and pull excludes them on both sides.
low
A secondary strict-interior mask, used only as a sensitivity check, keeps cells with phigh
T /p∥ ≤
◦
◦
tan 20 , removing the 20 reported cells that straddle the θµ < 20 kinematic cut. The 185-bin check
tests whether the main tension comes from the boundary. The main metric throughout uses the
205-bin set.
A.3.2

Covariance-aware χ2 /ndf

With ∆ ≡ xours − xpaper on the 205-bin set and C the chosen covariance,
χ2 = ∆⊤ C+ ∆,

ndf = nreported = 205,

χ2 /ndf = χ2 /205.

(17)

The comparison applies the pseudo-inverse of §A.1 to the 205 × 205 block and computes the quadratic
form. Options add OmniFold covariances via Eq. (16) or subtract the paper’s stat block to avoid
counting it alongside the bootstrap (§A.4).
Dimension-conditional ndf. Setting ndf = nreported requires nearly that many well-determined
directions. The choice rests on the 205-bin 2D truncation scan rather than being assumed: the
scan rises smoothly with the retained rank r, 0.39 (r=50) → 1.04 (100) → 1.76 (180) → 2.27 (205)
(exact-split cross-check: 0.69 → 2.35 → 3.30 → 3.66), with no plateau or late jump, indicating an
effective rank comparable to the dimension. This evidence is specific to 2D and does not transfer to
N-D covariances (§B.1).

covariances are scaled by the paper’s values. This is the definition used for the quoted value: of
five definitions recomputed, only this one reproduces 0.945. The others give 1.197 (our covariance
scaled by our own values), 0.958 (both scaled by ours), 0.963 (scaled by the geometric mean)
and 1.015 (ln(1 + C/(xi xj )) in place of the ratio). Adding the OmniFold error budget lowers the
summed-covariance distance from 2.27 to 0.929 per bin. It is a descriptive distance, not a validation
result, and three limits apply to every use of it.
(i) Missing cross-covariance prevents calibration. For estimates x and y from the same data,
Cov(x − y) = Cx + Cy − Cxy − Cyx ,

(18)

and the published IBU-era and OmniFold results share MINERvA data and flux, detector and
GENIE systematics. Without Cxy , the summed Cx + Cy double-counts shared systematics and omits
their correlation. Its net error has unknown size and sign. No value of the statistic, including 1.5 or
1, can be converted to a goodness-of-fit probability or a p-value.
(ii) Near-one realizations are not evidence ofp
correct uncertainties. Under calibrated assumptions,
χ2 /ndf has expectation 1 and spread of order 2/ndf, about 0.10 for ndf = 205. One realization
near 1, such as the quoted 0.929, is not evidence of correct uncertainties. Nor is the exact-split
cross-check’s 1.47, computed with an earlier covariance built before the flux-band repair, evidence
against them on its own (Ref. [37], superseded values).
(iii) It does not address coverage (§4.6, §5.2). The covariance and the central value share the
LightGBM estimator and, in the universe and statistical blocks, its training seed (§3.2). Agreement
claims use per-bin standardized differences and ratios (§A.3.3); this scalar is only a distance and
ratio to the paper. The log-normal value 0.945 depends on the definition given with the worked
example.
External statistical floor of the quoted combined χ2 . The quoted 0.929 inverts the sum of
the paper’s covariance and ours, not our blocks alone. That sum contains the paper’s StatOnlyCov,
which has rank 205 and is near-diagonal (§A.4), so it places a variance floor under every eigendirection
of the combined matrix. It is the larger of the two statistical contributions. Our statistical block is
0.407 of it in square-root trace, and the square of that factor in trace (operands in §A.4). Each ratio
states which power of the trace it uses, because the two ratios describe the same comparison and
differ by more than a factor of two. An external, published block, rather than any ensemble of ours,
therefore sets the small-eigenvalue directions on which a finite-N debiasing factor would act. This
bounds the quoted number’s exposure to declaration (v) of §B.1.
Trace share alone cannot justify this protection: trace weights eigenvalues by λ, precision by 1/λ,
so even a negligible-trace block can dominate an inverse. Without the external floor, our finite-N
blocks fill Csyst ’s null space (rank 138 → 205, eigenvalues above 10−12 λmax ) and, inverted directly,
yield χ2 /ndf = 22.29 (§A.4). Protection depends on the source of the smallest retained eigenvalues.

using only the paper covariance, with no OmniFold addition, for the exact-split cross-check (not

A.4

Ill-conditioning and rank deficiency

The obstruction. A data-to-model χ2 /ndf with this analysis’s covariance alone, χ2ours , requires
inverting Cours = Csyst + Cboot + CML + CPL instead of the paper’s V . Its universe block is
rank-deficient,
rank(Csyst
cond(Csyst ) = 9.78 × 1011 ,
(21)
sample ) = 138/205,
counting eigenvalues of (C + C⊤ )/2 above 10−12 λmax , with cond the ratio of λmax to the smallest
retained eigenvalue (124 and 9.45 × 109 above 10−10 λmax ). Every rank and condition number of our
blocks quoted below uses this definition. Band count, not universe count, limits the rank: Eq. (11)
gives each ±1σ pair rank 1 and the 100-universe PPFX band rank ≤ 99. PPFX is effectively rank-1
(§4.1). The 43 bands cannot span 205 bins. The deficit does not come from missing bands. The
paper’s systematic-only covariance (TotalCov − StatOnlyCov) has rank 120, below our 138. We
use the same GetStandardSystematics and propagate at least as many independent systematic
directions. The statistical block (StatOnlyCov, rank 205, near-diagonal) alone makes the paper’s V
full-rank (rank = 205, with 204 eigenvalues above 10−12 λmax ; cond = 1.5 × 1012 ). Our 300-replica
bootstrap similarly gives rank(Csyst +Cboot ) = 205 (eigenvalues above 10−12 λmax ; cond = 7.91×1011
by the definition above).
The real obstruction is statistical, not rank. Even at full rank (205) a defensible single χ2ours
is out of reach, for a statistical-precision reason.
in global scale:
√ The bootstrap block is smaller
√
its square-root trace is 0.407 of the paper’s ( TrCboot = 1.886 × 10−40 vs Tr StatOnlyCov =
4.639 × 10−40 ). The paper’s full-rank, near-diagonal StatOnlyCov supplies a variance floor in every
reported eigendirection; because the covariance structures differ, this should not be read as a uniform
per-mode effect. The direct inverse of Csyst + Cboot gives χ2 /ndf = 22.29 (21.77 for all four blocks)
for the LightGBM central value, against the paper covariance’s 2.27 with its larger stat floor, so a
usable χ2ours depends on the statistical block, not on more systematic bands. The gap is not a missing
MC-statistical term: our bootstrap already draws independent Poisson(1) multipliers on data, signal
MC and background MC per replica (§A.2.2; Practical Guide 2507.09582 §5.1). The remaining
candidates are a real difference in statistical efficiency between the unbinned reweighting and the
published binned D’Agostini unfold, a difference in what the paper’s StatOnlyCov encompasses, or a
regularization effect in our own estimator that suppresses dispersion without improving information.
A split bootstrap of the earlier rebuilt bootstrap (Ref. [37], superseded values: retired material)
separated the first two from an MC-statistics omission but not from the third, and it has not been
repeated for the quoted statistical block.

Each method spans roughly two decades of χ2 /205 across a two-decade scan of its regularization
parameter, with no defensible single cutoff. The useful conclusion is diagnostic, not a number: the
naive inverse is dominated by tiny, poorly-determined modes, and in the retained high-variance
modes the central-value difference is not large. We therefore report direct per-bin pulls (§A.3.3) and
this scan rather than a single unqualified χ2ours .
Bootstrap/paper-stat double-count. The combined distance (§A.3.2) adds Cboot to TotalCov,
double-counting the paper’s included statistical block. Subtracting that block uses TotalCov −
StatOnlyCov
as baseline before our additions, changing χ2 /ndf 0.929 → 6.72. It over-corrects: our
√
√
TrCboot = 1.886 × 10−40 is 0.407 of the paper’s Tr StatOnlyCov = 4.639 × 10−40 , leaving no
comparable replacement. The double-counted 0.929 is kept as the combined distance, a well-defined
measure of what OmniFold’s uncertainty estimate adds on top of the published V ; the subtraction
result is a secondary diagnostic.
Remaining refinements. The known band-independence exception, the Bashyal flux ↔ Muon_Energy_MINOS joint block, has been rederived (ρ = 0.900). It preserves systematic rank because
its directions already lie in the universe covariance’s span (§4.1). The quoted 2D covariance therefore
remains the block sum, with the cross term as a diagnostic. A fitted (Φnorm , Φshape , MuonEscale )
posterior from MAT-MINERvA could supply a paper-independent ρ. The collaboration uses the
same truncated-spectral pseudo-inverse as χ2ours (private communication), so the convention is
collaboration practice rather than one introduced by this analysis. No published reference for it has
been identified.
What is reported. The combined χ2 /ndf = 0.929 is the descriptive distance of §A.3.2, not a
quoted result or validation statistic, with the limits stated there (Eq. (18)). The per-band sum is
MAT-conformant, with the Flux band weighted as MAT weights it (the earlier machine-precision
check of §A.2.6 covers the unweighted bands) and the 1.4% normalization as a rank-1 band. The
truncated-spectral χ2ours uses Cours , which for the quoted covariance has rank 197 above 10−10 λmax ;
the regularization scan shown above is of an earlier Cours . The 2D agreement claims are the
total-cross-section ratio, per-bin ratio distribution and published-σ standardized differences; no
covariance-based goodness-of-fit is claimed.

A.5

Anatomy of the paper-covariance tension

This derivation supports §6.4. Its numbers are for the exact-split cross-check, the quoted 2D central
value when the derivation was made (§3.2). For the quoted LightGBM central value, §6.4 gives
the same decomposition from the same code: a smaller distance (2.27), an 8× rather than 10×
off-diagonal inflation, 9.6 % rather than 3 % in the ten smallest-λ modes, and its leading poorly
determined modes in the highest-p∥ bins, while its moderate-λ modes remain at low-to-moderate pT .

reproduces the pseudo-inverse χ2 = 750.49 to 3 × 10−9 . The tension is neither a few pathological null
directions nor the absolute smallest eigenvalues. The largest contributions lie at λk /λmax ∼ 10−4 –
10−5 , in the lower-middle spectrum, ∼ 7–8 decades above the condition floor (λ/λmax ∼ 10−12 ).
Their standardized-component magnitudes 5–6 are descriptive coordinates in the published basis,
without calibrated significance for independent results. The ten smallest-λ modes carry only 3% of
the χ2 and about 90 modes are needed to reach 90%: the χ2 is broadly distributed, not localized
to the ill-conditioned tail. A scan of the pseudo-inverse cutoff excludes a numerical artifact: the
73 best-determined directions (λk /λmax > 10−4 ) give χ2 = 291 (χ2 /205 = 1.42), and 139 directions
(> 10−6 ) give χ2 = 572 (χ2 /205 = 2.79), each divided by the number of reported bins, not by the
number of directions kept. The rank-truncation scan of §A.3.2 rises smoothly with no late jump, so
the tension does not depend on the last few least-determined modes.
Shape contribution. Splitting ∆ along xpaper and its orthogonal remainder gives χ2norm = 0.10
for scale, χ2shape = 750.1 for shape, and cross term 0.28; the split is not C+ -orthogonal. The +1.1%
normalization offset is statistically irrelevant to the tension; it is a correlated shape difference,
consistent with Jacobian shape-only χ2 /ndf = 3.60 (§A.3.4).
Location in the (pT , p∥ ) plane. Mapping dominant eigenvectors and per-bin contributions
∆i (C+ ∆)i to the 14 × 16 grid (numbers for the exact-split cross-check; Figs. 11 and 12 show the
LightGBM central value) locates tension on the low-pT peak ridge (pT ≲ 0.4 GeV/c, pT -bins 2/7/10
carry 16/11/12%) and low-p∥ near-edge cells (p∥ -bins 0/1/8). Carrier modes oscillate in sign across
the peak. They form neither vertical pT -columns characteristic of a flux 1/Φ(pT ) normalization error
nor the θµ < 20◦ acceptance boundary. Flux and Muon_Energy, the largest bands and only named
cross-band correlation (Bashyal, §A.4), dominate there. Tight bin-to-bin correlations leave little
freedom to absorb a coherent ∼ 1–2% shape difference, so a small OmniFold-vs-IBU shape difference
becomes a large χ2 .2

A methodological component (estimator and iteration). Changing the GBDT backend
on the same data and normalization (Σσ within 0.1%) shifts the χ2 against the paper covariance
by ∼ 1 unit. The integrated agreement does not mean the bins agree: the LightGBM seed-1 and
exact-split central values differ by a median of 0.97 % per bin, 1.3 statistical standard deviations
(seed 42: 0.98 %, 1.32; §3.2). Iteration-count effects are negligible,

All rows except the quoted one use the purity-based subtraction. (i) Iteration count is negligible:

is ∼ 25× smaller than the 0.96 exact→hist gap, so that gap is attributable to the estimator, not
to seed noise). The exact-gradient trees fit finer shape structure (less implicit smoothing) and so
deviate more from the paper’s IBU-regularized result. This is direct evidence that ∼ 1 χ2 -unit of
the exact-split cross-check’s tension is due to OmniFold’s implicit regularization rather than to a
physical data–MC disagreement.
Conclusion. For the exact-split cross-check, the tension is a broadly distributed, correlated
shape difference concentrated on the low-pT peak dominated by flux/Muon_Energy bands; for
the LightGBM central value the distance is smaller and a larger share lies in poorly determined
directions (§6.4). Normalization and small-λ inversion artifacts do not drive it. A ∼ 1-χ2 -unit
GBDT estimator/iteration regularization band adds a methodological component. Both point to the
same structure: the Bashyal flux ↔ Muon_Energy joint block (§A.4) is the covariance structure that
governs the region carrying the tension.

A.6

Closure tests: detecting bias

The variance methods of §A.2 quantify the scatter of the unfolded result. Closure tests measure its
bias, a consistent offset from a known injected truth, which the variance methods cannot show. Each
of three tests injects a known MC deformation, unfolds, and compares the median per-bin |residual|
of the recovered answer with a threshold. The thresholds lie comfortably above the 0.166% ML floor
of the earlier ten-seed scan, which was in force when the tests were graded (Ref. [37], superseded
values: retired material). All use playlist-1A LightGBM 5-iteration unfolds. Thresholds and results
are in §5; procedures and otherwise unreported values follow.
A.6.1

Truth-reweight closure

For known f (pT , p∥ ), both reco and truth MC event weights are multiplied by f . Reweighted
pseudo-data are unfolded with the unchanged CV response and
 compared with the
 reweighted
truth marginal. Two shapes are tested: gauss_pt, f = 1 + A exp − ((pT − p0T )/σ)2 with A = 0.2,

ref
p0T = 0.4 GeV/c, σ = 0.1 GeV/c; and tilt_pz, f = 1 + α(p∥ − pref
∥ ) with α = 0.1, p∥ = 5 GeV/c.
Both residuals are at least 30× below the threshold: OmniFold recovers the injected truth shape.

A.6.2

Hidden-variable closure

OmniFold sees reco and truth (pT , p∥ ) but excludes resolution variable ∆pT ≡ preco
− ptruth
. A bias
T
T
confined to this hidden axis tests leakage into the measured marginal. Pseudo-data receive



wreco → wreco · 1 + A exp − ((∆pT − c)/σ∆pT )2 ,
(24)
with c = +0.1 GeV/c, σ∆pT = 0.05 GeV/c, and amplitude scanned over A ∈ {0.05, 0.10, 0.30}. Truth
weights are unchanged, so the reference is the unaltered CV truth marginal. For median per-bin
signed residual r̄(A), a linear fit gives r̄(A) ≈ κleak A, with κleak = ∂ r̄/∂A, the fraction of hidden
∆pT bias entering the visible answer. The 3.0% acceptance bound is looser than the truth-reweight
bound because the weak ∆pT –pT correlation prevents complete removal of the leakage.
On the 205 reported bins the per-amplitude median residuals, 0.57%/1.14%/3.43% for A =
0.05/0.10/0.30, are linear in A, as expected for small perturbations, with fitted slope κleak = 0.113 :
about 11% of a hidden ∆pT bias leaks into the measured (pT , p∥ ) marginal. Against the 3.0%
acceptance bound, two of the three arms pass and the largest does not: A = 0.30 gives 3.43%,
which exceeds 3.0% (§5). The scan establishes the linearity and the coefficient: the acceptance
bound is satisfied for hidden-∆pT deformations up to roughly A ≃ 0.26 and is violated above it. No
non-closure uncertainty is assigned from this scan either way (§5). A ∆pT -shaped mismodeling at
the 30% level would leak more than the analysis declared tolerable; this is an open sensitivity, not a
passing test.
A.6.3

Alternative-model closure

The OmniFold response is trained on the CV weights and the pseudo-data are built from an alternativeuniverse reweighting: MaCCQE:0 (+1σ), which probes an interaction-model shape change, or Flux:50
(PPFX index 50), which probes flux reweighting — two distinct kinds of model dependence. The
reference is the in-acceptance-extrapolated alternative truth,
X̃iref = XiCV,truth ·

XiCV,in-acc

(25)

scaling the CV truth marginal by the alternative-to-CV in-acceptance count ratio to isolate modelshape response from acceptance/efficiency effects. In-acceptance biases are −1.68% for MaCCQE:0,
removed by extrapolation, and −7.32% for Flux:50. Both residuals are below the 2.0% bound by at
least a factor of 5, showing robustness to model variations of order ±1σ GENIE and full PPFX flux
universes.

Across 205 reported bins, combined OmniFold + paper uncertainties give χ2 /ndf = 0.929 and pull
mean/RMS 0.034/0.388. This distance is descriptive, without calibrated goodness-of-fit (§A.3.2).
Same-ensemble toy pulls are approximately Gaussian; the 0.048% training-seed block is more than
an order of magnitude below the systematic block.

Standing assumptions
1. Pulls and χ2 approximate bins as Gaussian: weighted sums of O(104 ) MC events justify the
central-limit approximation.
2. Independence of error sources. Eq. (16) requires no cross-correlations between ML,
statistical, systematic and run-period blocks. The bootstrap pins the GBDT seed (so ML noise
cancels at first order); universes pin the seed too; the training-seed block averages the same
20 bootstrap replicas at each seed setting and includes the bootstrap × seed interaction at its
1/20 share. Among the first three, cross-terms are higher-order. The run-period block is built
from separate unfolds of the same data, and its noise share is not subtracted (§4.5).
3. Same-ensemble 68.71% (§5.4) checks Gaussianity; it does not certify Cstat at the half-percent
level. The fixed-truth test (§5.2) found the diagonal of the first-built Cstat undercovering in
its tails (C2 = 0.912, unevenly across bins); the band was rebuilt in response, and the rebuilt
band has not been independently re-tested. The statistical block quoted here is a different
ensemble and has not been tested either.
4. Band independence in Csyst . Different physics sources are taken as uncorrelated; the one
known exception, the Bashyal flux ↔ muon-E joint block, is treated in §A.4.
5. Closure passes apply to tested perturbations; unphysical deformations far outside training
have no guarantee of passing.

B

Statistical methods for the multidimensional results and inference

This appendix gives the conventions for covariances and comparisons beyond the 2D result: rank,
ndf and finite-ensemble declarations for N-D covariances, the scalar five-dimensional diagnostic
reductions and interval definitions, the extended-fiducial band and pull fractions, and inference from
unfolded events and selected projections. The 2D methods and their worked examples are in App. A.

B.1

Rank, ndf and finite-ensemble declarations for N-D covariances

The 2D justification in §A.3.2 does not transfer to N-D. An earlier 5D covariance from the standard
production chain has 10 694 reported bins, effective positive rank 263 and infinite numerical condition
number. It is distinct from CZ , the five-axis covariance of the configuration-(a) central value, and
was built before CZ was chosen for use. Its 4825-bin 5D→4D projection retains rank 263 exactly.

The covariance is a sum of about 45 rank-one outer products, one for each two-endpoint MAT
systematic band, and of the statistical and machine-learning (ML) sample blocks; such a sum cannot
span 10 694 directions, so the rank deficiency follows from the construction. Quoting χ2 /nreported
would understate tension by roughly 40× in 5D or 18× in 4D.
For an N-D covariance, ndf is the retained rank, not nreported . Every N-D χ2 must state, where
it is quoted, (i) the inverse actually used, including the pseudo-inverse rcond or the truncation rank;
(ii) the retained rank, as ndf; (iii) the rank-truncation scan of that covariance, which is expected
to lack the smoothness that supports the 2D choice in §A.3.2; (iv) the identity of the covariance,
since the earlier 5D covariance, its 4D projection and the published 2D block differ in rank; and
(v) for each sample block in the sum, the ensemble size N , the biased 1/N or unbiased 1/(N − 1)
normalization, the effective inverted dimension p after truncation, and the finite-ensemble treatment,
or that none is applied. The measurements, the derivation and the requirement that N-D difference
statistics use a rank cut fixed in advance are in Ref. [37] (R1.15).
Finite-ensemble bias of the inverse. Item (v) records the ensemble size of each block; without it,
an uncorrected finite-ensemble bias could enter unnoticed. The inverse of a noisy sample covariance
is biased upward. For an unbiased 1/(N − 1) estimator over N independent Gaussian realizations,
the standard debiasing factor is (N − p − 2)/(N − 1). For this analysis’s production convention,
universe-mean centered with biased 1/N normalization, it is (N − p − 2)/N . The superseded
unified-throw 5D covariance has N = 160, truncated p = 100, giving 58/160 ≃ 0.363: uncorrected
precision is too large by ≃ 2.76. For fixed residuals this inflates χ2 and overstates tension; confidence
regions from the same over-tight covariance become over-optimistic.
No generic correction is prescribed. A sum of deterministic rank-one band terms and statistical
and ML sample blocks, such as the earlier 5D covariance from the standard production chain, has
no single debiasing factor. The standard factor also assumes independent Gaussian realizations
and a data-independent truncation dimension, neither established for a data-dependent cut. The
appropriate treatment depends on the covariance and inverse actually used, and is to be determined
from the quantities recorded under item (v).

B.2

Scalar-5D diagnostic reductions and interval definitions

The synthesis in §9.4 reuses saved outputs; it involves no new unfolding, training or pseudoexperiments. Fine-grid cross-section densities are converted to integrals by multiplying by all five bin
widths before aggregation. The saved trace functionals on the EW cells are differential in (Eavail , W )
and are multiplied by the cell areas; those on the J and H2 cells are already integrals. Direct
tensor-slice integration checks the index-map sums and full-domain rate. Reporting boundaries
coincide with fine edges. The J boundaries are pT = (0, 0.55, 0.85, 4.5), p∥ = (1.5, 3.5, 6, 60),
Eavail = (0, 0.4, 1.5, 100), q3 = (0, 1.2, 2, 100) and W = (0, 1.4, 2.2, 100); H2 uses interior boundaries
(0.7, 4.5, 0.8, 2, 1.8), respectively, with the same outer boundaries. Energy units are GeV and
momentum units GeV/c. A cell is supported when both its expected reconstructed signal and
selected truth weight at data exposure are at least 1000. This gives 109 J, 27 H2 and 39 EW cells;
the unsupported EW11, EW36 and EW37 are omitted from the supported-cell summaries only.

The development assessment includes an interval diagnostic. It uses σf , the sample standard

without bias correction or model envelope. The table gives integer hits, both empirical fractions,

estimated bootstrap widths without propagating finite-replica uncertainty. Coverage fractions are
not pooled across cells to certify them. The examples shown are a populated low-energy cell (EW7)
and the corner cell EW41, already known to fail; they are not freshly selected validation targets.

within half a float32 unit in the last place. With 20 base jitters, 50 unperturbed bootstrap replicas

Its denominator differs from the simulated residual’s. Neither hf nor an outer product of closure
residuals is given a probability model. The reductions keep the per-cell values, the identity of each
input and the exclusions, and distinguish complete outputs from checkpoints that carry no metadata.
From a checkpoint, only the iterations that reproduce the originally recorded values are used; later
entries are excluded. The analyst cross-checked the reductions numerically.

B.3

Extended-fiducial statistical-band and pull fractions

For the containment diagnostic in §12, the independent scalar statistical-covariance band contains
77.6 % of closure bin–toy cells and exceeds the closure-toy spread. The same-ensemble pull fraction is
68.9 %, with mean absolute pull ⟨|r|⟩ ≈ 0.79; it remains a diagnostic of the pulls and their Gaussianity.
The split-sample fractions in the published and extension regions are 68.5 % and 70.0 %, respectively;
both are descriptive, and neither is used to certify a covariance.

B.4

Inference from unfolded events and selected projections

Unfolding correlates weighted events. Treating them as independent in a downstream likelihood can
underestimate parameter uncertainties [63]; nominal weights do not specify uncertainty. Coherent
unfolding replicas could propagate it through the fit. Resampling unfolded rows independently is
not equivalent to resampling inputs and repeating the learned estimator. Classifier and permutation goodness-of-fit tests also require null calibration for their fitting and data reuse. These are
requirements on such inference, with no new validation result here.
The uncertainty target matters: separate bin or projection coverage does not imply simultaneous
coverage. A fixed linear projection y = P x propagates covariance as Cy = P Cx P ⊤ , with integrated

bin widths in P for densities. The identity neither calibrates that covariance nor accounts for regions
chosen after inspecting data.
Recent work constructs confidence intervals for selected functionals in constrained inverse problems [64] and joint confidence regions for multiple functionals [65], which could provide independent
benchmarks for specified integrals or contrasts. Their guarantees depend on the forward model, noise
law, and parameter constraints and do not transfer automatically to a learned response with finite
simulation and detector nuisances. A rank-deficient forward operator is also distinct from a rankdeficient sample covariance; neither truncating the latter nor validating a few projections certifies
the full distribution. Such a benchmark would supplement, not replace, the scalar five-dimensional
uncertainty construction that a measurement would require.

B.5

Familywise error of the sequential joint-test decisions

This derivation supports the familywise statement of §11.3.
Probability model. Fix one test, and condition on the data and on the simulated sample.
The calibration pseudo-experiments, taken in their fixed seed order, give exceedance indicators
Xb = 1[Tb ≥ tobs ]. Assumption A2 is that, given the data, these are independent Bernoulli draws
with a common probability p, the calibrated tail probability of the observed statistic; this requires
the lost pseudo-experiments to be lost independently of their outcomes. Assumption A1 is that, for
a true null, p is super-uniform over repetitions of the experiment, P (p ≤ u) ≤ u, with the nuisance
parameters drawn from their priors; the size statement is therefore averaged over the nuisance priors.
Let kB be the number of exceedances among the first B draws and U (k, B) the upper end of the
two-sided 95% Clopper–Pearson interval, a one-sided 97.5% upper bound.
Crossing event. The rule rejects at threshold c only if U (kB , B) < c at the number B ≤ 1999
at which sampling stopped. Whatever rule chose that stopping point, including stops decided
with results visible,
S irregular looks, lost draws, and the budget extensions, the rejection lies in
the event Ec = B≤1999 {U (kB , B) < c} that the bound crosses below c at some B. Hence
P (reject at c) ≤ R(c) ≡ Ep [gc (p)], with gc (p) = P (Ec | p).
Uniform p is the worst case. Coupling the Bernoulli draws in p shows that gc (p) does not
increaseRwith p. For any super-uniform p its expectation is therefore at most its value for uniform p,
1
R(c) ≤ 0 gc (u) du.
The decision rule, operationally. The tests are ordered by increasing (k + 1)/(B + 1), with
ties broken in the fixed listing order (total before shape for each null). At step i the threshold
is α/(m − i + 1): the test is rejected if U < α/(m − i + 1), it is not rejected if the lower end of
its interval exceeds the threshold, and it is undetermined otherwise; a test that is not rejected or
undetermined ends the step-down, and the tests after it take the same status. Equality U = c does
not reject.
Holm reduction without independence. Let m0 of the m = 10 hypotheses be true. The first true
null to be rejected is reached after at most m − m0 rejections of false nulls, so its threshold is at
most α/m0 . A familywise error therefore requires some true null to cross below α/m0 , and by the
union bound FWER ≤ m0 R(α/m0 ) ≤ maxj≤m j R(α/j). The argument uses neither independence
between tests nor the order of the Holm steps, so the shared ensemble of a null’s total and shape
tests, their unequal stopping counts, and the ranking by (k + 1)/(B + 1) with its ties do not affect it.
The decision p value is the largest over shifted nulls that include the unshifted one, evaluated at the
same B, so its exceedance count is at least that of the unshifted test; since U increases with k, the
bound for the unshifted test covers the decision rule.
Certified constant. For each c = α/j, j = 1, . . . , 10, let k ∗ (B) be the largest k with U (k, B) < c.
For fixed p, a dynamic program propagates the distribution of kB from B = 1 to 1999, moving

the probability of every state with kB ≤ k ∗ (B) into an absorbing “crossed” state at each B, and
pooling the counts above maxB k ∗ (B), which can never cross because counts never decrease; the
absorbed probability is gc (p) exactly. This is evaluated on a grid of 6000 points in [0, 6c]. Because gc
is nonincreasing, a left Riemann sum over-estimates its Rintegral on each grid interval, and with the
1
tail term (1 − 6c) gc (6c) it is a rigorous upper bound on 0 gc , so the integration error is included in
the bound. The largest ratio R(c)/c, at j = 1, lies in [0.8892, 0.8902]; it decreases to [0.5138, 0.5148]
at j = 10. Hence, for α = 0.05, the value used here, FWER ≤ 0.8902 α < 0.9 α under any stopping
at or before 1999 pseudo-experiments. The bound rests on A1, the calibration’s fidelity, which §11.4
discusses, and on A2.

C

Unbinned background subtraction by negative-weight injection

Binned purity subtraction (§3.1) is the only step that uses analysis binning before final histogramming.
Each data event in reconstructed bin b receives the purity weight wpur (b) = max(0, Ndata (b) −
Nbkg (b))/Ndata (b). Injecting background simulation on the measured side with negative weight gives
a fully unbinned correction with the same bin-integrated estimand. Its agreement with the binned
correction is quantified on the 2D measurement below. It is the continuous version of the purity
weight recommended for nontrivial backgrounds in Ref. [18], and the natural treatment for the larger
backgrounds of the extended-fiducial analysis.
Scope of the numbers. The numbers in this appendix are not quoted uncertainties, and the
analytic derivation, the estimand identity and the displacement argument do not depend on any.
Which background treatment each quoted result uses is stated in §3.1, and by configuration in
§3.2 (Table 2). Negative-weight injection is studied here for its equivalence to binned purity and
as the extended-fiducial treatment; this appendix establishes no supported production path and
changes no default. The two covariance ratios are agreement diagnostics between two realizations of
one subtraction. Two provenance limits apply to the production numbers: the driver revision that
produced them is corroborated but not pinned, and no output records the job that produced them
(Ref. [37], R1.7).

C.1

Estimator

OmniFold step 1 trains a classifier to separate simulated reconstruction (class 0, weighted density S)
from the measured sample (class 1, weighted density ρ1 ), then reweights simulation by the learned
density ratio r(x) = p(x)/(1 − p(x)). Its population optimum is r⋆ (x) = ρ1 (x)/S(x). Background
treatment is entirely a choice of ρ1 :
• binned purity assigns every data event in reconstructed analysis bin b the same scalar. For
x ∈ b,

• negative-weight injection appends reconstructed background-simulation events to the measured

ρneg

pointwise, without analysis binning.
118

(29)

Equations (28) and (29) differ as functions of x. A bin-constant rescaling reproduces D(x) − B(x)
inside bin b only when B/D is constant there. Otherwise, D(x) (1 − Bb /Db ) and D(x) − B(x) share
a bin integral but differ within the bin. The constructions agree exactly on bin masses under the
piecewise-constant sieve of §C.2; in general they differ by within-bin shape terms. Both target
the bin-integrated estimand r⋆ (b) = (Db − Bb )/Sb ; they do not share the continuous estimand
r⋆ (x) = (D(x) − B(x))/S(x). §C.4 and §C.5 measure the effect of that difference. The injected
background and data occupy the same reconstructed fiducial window, giving identical support.
The floor. Where D − B < 0, binned purity floors the correction at zero through max(0, ·). The
unbinned classifier’s bounded output p ∈ (0, 1) forces r = p/(1 − p) ≥ 0. Non-negativity is structural,
while the value at the floor follows from the bounded output and ordinary regularization rather than
from a minimizer of the fit (§C.2). Locally negative class-1 mass makes the fit ill-posed and calls for
a positive-target prescription. We apply the “Stay Positive” refinement of Ref. [66], then explicitly
zero-clip the learned refinement factor before step-1 training. Agreement between plain injection
and clipped refinement depends on the classifier.
With the histogram-based gradient-boosted classifier used in this comparison, they agree to the
reported median per-bin ratio 1.000 at this measurement’s sub-percent genuine-background scale.
This histogram backend is neither the LightGBM estimator of the quoted 2D central value and its
ensembles nor the exact-split cross-check (§3). The equivalence is therefore established on a third
backend, and its transfer to the production configuration is assumed rather than demonstrated. Raw
signed weights destabilize an exact-split boosted-tree classifier, inflating a full low-pT cross-section
row by up to a factor ∼5 × 104 where local background is large. Clipped Stay-Positive refinement
removes this pathology and recovers binned-purity agreement to a median 0.999 (141/148 bins
within 5 %). This clipped refinement is therefore what makes the unbinned subtraction robust across
estimators, and it matters most for the larger, more structured backgrounds of the extended-fiducial
analysis.

C.2

Binned limit: exact reduction to the purity correction

A step-1 classifier restricted to functions piecewise-constant on reconstructed analysis bins {b} gives
the population-level binned limit of the OmniFold/Richardson–Lucy correspondence in Refs. [1,
2]. Its output is a single p per bin. With common support and unnormalized class weights, step 1
weighted cross-entropy separates into per-bin objectives
Lb (p) = −W1 ln p − W0 ln(1 − p),

(30)

where W0 = S(b) > 0 is the integral of simulated class-0 density S in bin b, and W1 is the signed
class-1 weight. Injecting background simulation at negative weight while keeping data at +1 gives
W1 = D(b) − B(b). Here D(b) and B(b) are the bin masses of D and B, equivalently Ndata (b) and
Nbkg (b) in §C.1.
For D(b) > B(b), objective (30) is strictly convex on (0, 1) with unique interior minimizer

The max(0, ·) floor is a prescription, not a minimizer’s value. A finite regularized classifier’s behavior
depends on the estimator. Stay-Positive remains well behaved but can retain negative refined
weights where the target density is genuinely negative. The zero clip used here is therefore an
additional analysis prescription chosen to match the physical purity floor. Ref. [66] documents the
same reweighting-loss divergence for negative targets; signed binary cross-entropy has an analogous
pathology for sWeights [67].
The binned D − B update, negative-weight classification of the measured side, and binned
OmniFold/Richardson–Lucy correspondence already appear together in Ref. [2]. Equation (31)
states their immediate binwise corollary and its precise validity domain for this analysis. It is a
consistency check, not a separate method.

C.3

Closed-form toy

A one-dimensional toy uses signal simulation N (0, 1), equally normalized data signal N (0.3, 1.1),
and background N (2, 0.7) contributing 16.7 % of pseudo-data. The known target (D − B)/S =
ϕ(x; 0.3, 1.1)/ϕ(x; 0, 1) is smooth and positive. A signed-weight gradient-boosted classifier recovers
it with median 6.8 % relative error, matching binned purity’s 6.7 % and reproducing it bin-for-bin
on the same problem. Omitting subtraction leaves 10.9 % residual-background bias. Results are
stable across classifier seeds (±0.3 %), with no numerical instability from negative weights. Moving
background into the sparse signal tail and deliberately over-subtracting makes the learned reweight
floor cleanly at zero where B > D, as anticipated.

C.4

Comparison on the 2D measurement

With identical unfolding settings, the histogram backend and a common classifier seed, the ME FHC
comparison isolates background treatment (Table 34). Total cross sections agree to −0.13 %, an
order of magnitude inside the ∼3 % purity correction. The per-bin ratio has median 1.000 and 1.4 %
RMS scatter across the plane (Fig. 35). Only one low-pT , high-background edge bin differs by more
than a few percent (−12.6 %); background concentrates there and subtraction is largest. Unbinned
subtraction reproduces the binned correction wherever it is well determined. Departures occur only
where the binned max(0, ·) and structural floor of §C.1 govern the result.

C.5

Propagation through systematics and statistics

Replacing binned subtraction requires reproducing its uncertainty budget as well as the central value.
The full 2D ME FHC covariance was rebuilt end to end with negative-weight injection and compared
to binned purity at identical settings, at full statistics with LightGBM, the estimator of the quoted
2D central value. The 187-universe systematic ensemble varied genuine background consistently
within each universe instead of freezing it at nominal; a statistical bootstrap used matched seeds.
Agreement is at the level predicted by the (D −√B)/S identity. The two covariances differ by one
to two percent. The 187-universe systematic Tr ratio (negative-weight/purity) is 0.986, from
2.9828 × 10−39 cm2 /nucleon and 3.0242 × 10−39 cm2 /nucleon, and the matched-seed statistical ratio
is 0.982, from 1.7260 × 10−40 cm2 /nucleon and 1.7576 × 10−40 cm2 /nucleon. Residuals of 1.4 % and
1.8 % are consistent with “to within 2 %” in §3. They are bootstrap and classifier-seed sampling
noise, with no method bias. Negative-weight injection reproduces the central cross section per bin
(§C.4) and the covariance in aggregate. The covariance comparison gives two trace ratios; per-bin,
correlation and eigenmode equivalence are not established.
The 2D central-value, closed-form recovery and full covariance comparisons, together with StayPositive robustness on larger extended-fiducial backgrounds, support negative-weight injection as an
unbinned alternative at the 2D scale. The 2D result and its covariance, the 3D and 4D central values
and the sixteen-cell five-dimensional result (§10) use its refined form (§3.1); binned purity remains
the treatment of the configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value and its five-axis covariance and
of the extended-fiducial value. Negative-weight injection is the designated extended-fiducial baseline
(§12), where binning-independence and the continuous floor are essential.

C.6

Which background treatment each production chain uses

Agreement between the two subtractions does not make the choice between them arbitrary, and the
production chains choose differently.
The standard N -dimensional chain uses purity. The entire standard five-dimensional chain,
that of the configuration-(a) central value and its five-axis covariance, uses binned purity (§3.1):
the central unfold, 169 vertical-band unfolds, 18 detector unfolds and 10 endpoint unfolds of the
re-selected lateral bands.
The existing endpoint unfolds, produced in July 2026, used purity, as established by elimination
from their job scripts and logs (Ref. [37], R1.17). Current production fixes the background treatment
in a configuration object that rejects every other mode. The standard job script passes the driver’s
background-mode option (--bkg-mode) explicitly from that configuration, and the value is written
into the record of every endpoint unfold as it is produced, so the background treatment of each new
component can be read from the component itself; this changes how the treatment is recorded, not
the treatment itself.
The standard chain uses purity by choice, on two grounds (Ref. [37], R1.8). First, a covariance
that mixes background treatments is inferior to one that uses either treatment consistently: a
lateral-band block built with negative-weight subtraction would be inconsistent with central and
vertical-band unfolds built with purity subtraction. Second, switching would have required rerunning
every unfold above and would have invalidated the flux-corrected covariance then just chosen for use;
this consequence, rather than the computing cost, decided the choice. The background treatment
has been revisited in two dimensions: the closure test below is its result, and the 2D result and its
covariance use the refined estimator. For the configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value, which
still uses purity, the revisit is open; the sixteen-cell result (§10) uses the refined estimator.
A two-dimensional closure test with backgrounds. Twenty pseudo-experiments at nominal
truth (the truth model equals the prior), with background included and drawn as in the fivedimensional pseudo-experiments, were unfolded in two dimensions with the purity subtraction
and with refined negative-weight subtraction (a 400-tree, 31-leaf refinement), both on the same
pseudo-data. Differences are expressed in units of the statistical and total standard deviations, σstat
and σtotal , of the purity-based 2D covariance on its 205 reported cells (the quoted covariance when
the test ran). The mean difference between the two treatments exceeds 1 σstat in 14 of the 205 cells,
at most 5.45 σstat , in the cell 0.70 ≤ pT < 0.85 GeV/c, 1.5 ≤ p∥ < 2.0 GeV/c. These cells are the
whole lowest-p∥ row (1.5–2.0 GeV/c) and five cells along the high-pT , low-p∥ boundary. In them,
the purity estimate’s mean residual against the truth is +0.78 to +5.89 σstat , above 1 σtotal in one
cell (1.22); the refined estimate’s mean residual is within ±0.44 σstat in every cell. The integrated
difference between the treatments is −0.198 %. In the median cell, the pseudo-experiments’ own
spread is 0.46 σstat (purity) and 0.58 σstat (refined). The test is a closure with 20 pseudo-experiments,
not a coverage test. On the data, the refined treatment moves the 2D result by at most 0.497 σtotal
per cell (median 0.060), by more than 1 σstat in 43 of the 205 cells (at most 4.26 σstat ), and by a
median of 0.44 % per cell (84th percentile 1.13 %, at most 10.1 %); its integral moves by −0.139 %.
The 2D result quoted in this note uses the refined treatment, configuration (f), with its covariance
and its comparison with the published result rebuilt for it (§6).
What the quoted extended-fiducial number used. The σext = 4.502 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon in
§12 was not produced with negative-weight injection. It comes from the extended-fiducial ME FHC
battery of 2026-06-10. Its ten endpoint unfolds used the purity correction and are recorded as purity

controls, not publication inputs (Ref. [37], R1.9). Its central value, 4.502 × 10−38 cm2 /nucleon, is
recorded with its passed published-region check against the control. The extended-fiducial central
value was therefore produced with the binned purity correction, not with refined negative-weight
subtraction, the intended extended-fiducial baseline.
The extended-fiducial analysis and its control are designated different background
treatments. The extended-fiducial analysis designates negative-weight injection with Stay-Positive
refinement as its background treatment. The code fixes it, and a check stops the run if any endpoint’s
recorded mode differs or is missing; the number quoted in §12 was not produced this way. The
standard chain uses purity throughout. Each side of the analysis and matched-control pair therefore
has an internally consistent background treatment; uniformity across the two sides is not claimed.

C.7

Displacement argument and scope of the agreement

Displacement across universes. The displacement argument rests on a measured input; it
assumes no identity of continuous estimands. The constructions share bin masses in the binned
limit (§C.2) but differ by within-bin shape terms (§C.1). Switching background treatment produces
a measured net per-universe displacement of order 0.1 %, including residual and shape effects, at
this measurement’s sub-percent genuine-background scale. Systematic covariance measures spread
across universes about their own mean. Similar small shifts of each universe leave that spread
nearly unchanged, explaining the 187-universe ratio 0.986, far from the purity correction’s ∼3 %.
This argument connects a handful-of-universes comparison to the 187-universe covariance; the full
187-universe rebuild in §C.5 tests it at 2D and passes.
Scope. The measured agreement concerns the 2D systematic and statistical covariances of §C.5 at
the stated configurations. It is not a theorem that the two background treatments give the same
covariance, because the underlying estimands are not pointwise identical, and covariance equivalence
for the continuous estimator or in five dimensions is not established. The five-dimensional evidence
is limited to the following two-universe spot check.
Limits of the five-dimensional evidence. There is no full five-dimensional 187-universe comparison of the two background treatments at the publication configuration (5 iterations, LightGBM). The
spot check uses one iteration and the histogram backend on the stored five-dimensional universe file:
under both treatments 2p2h:0 gives 3.014 × 10−38 with per-bin median 1.0005, and MaCCQE:0 differs
by −0.03 % with median 0.9990. Together with the displacement argument and the full-statistics
2D result, this supports quoting the standard chain with purity subtraction, with its background
treatment stated. It does not show that the background treatment is irrelevant in five dimensions,
which would require the full comparison. Raw negative weights destabilize the exact-split backend
(§C.1), but the standard chain uses LightGBM, so neither treatment is exposed to that instability
here.

C.8

Five-dimensional pseudo-experiments with both background treatments

The five-dimensional validation (§§9.1–9.3) reports end-to-end tests of both background treatments.
They cover the purity bias in the highest-W column at nominal truth, its reduction with refined
negative-weight subtraction, the data difference, and the estimator with higher refinement capacity.
That estimator is configuration (e), which gives the 3D and 4D central values and the sixteen-cell

result (§10) but not the configuration-(a) 5D central value; the quoted 2D central value uses the
same refinement capacity, in configuration (f).
Purity bias. Purity bias is 8–12 statistical standard deviations; signal-only experiments close.
Subtraction is exact per reconstructed bin, but the widest bin (W ≥ 3 GeV, background 13 % of
signal) contains background shape resolved by the unbinned classifier and missed by bin-uniform
purity. The within-bin shape term of §C.1 is therefore measurable, and it is direct evidence for
revisiting the background treatment of the configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value (§C.6).
The difference between five-dimensional purity and refined negative-weight subtraction on data is
the least computationally expensive estimate of its size on data.
Refined negative-weight subtraction. Refinement used the unfold’s deterministic GBDT
configuration, independent injection and subtraction halves of background simulation, and bootstrap
replicas that individually resampled every pseudo-event and refit refinement. It preserves the signed
total to 3 × 10−6 and clips 0.01 % to 0.05 % of events. Corner bias falls from 1.66 % to 0.24 %,
but residuals in some joint cells fail the nominal closure criterion. The criterion allows none of
the 153 reported quantities a mean residual beyond 3.6 standard errors of the mean (|t| > 3.6,
the two-sided 5% level, Bonferroni-corrected over the 153), or beyond half its bootstrap standard
deviation with |t| > 3. The data difference’s sign pattern matches simulated purity bias at an order
of magnitude smaller size; the difference is method sensitivity, not a measured bias. With either
treatment, the estimator does not follow generator-sized Eavail shape changes. No coverage test was
run. Reducing one bias does not itself qualify an uncertainty, so this result does not close the revisit
of the background treatment of the configuration-(a) five-dimensional central value (§C.6).
Refinement capacity. Stay-Positive classifier underfitting causes the residual nominal bias of
the refined subtraction. At the unfold’s GBDT setting (100 trees, 8 leaves), the positive-weight
probability is underfit, leaving up to 4 % reco-level subtraction error per (Eavail , W ) reco cell. With
400 trees and 31 leaves, error falls to 1.7 %, paired truth-level bias falls by more than 80 % in
every affected cell, and nominal background-inclusive closure passes on 20 development and 40 fresh
pseudo-experiments. Splitting background simulation into independent injection and subtraction
halves is not the cause.
Background treatment and choice of covariance. The choice of background treatment is
independent of the choice of covariance, and this appendix selects no five-dimensional covariance
for use. The lateral-band component from re-selected event loops is built, with five bands and ten
endpoint unfolds whose distinct checksums are listed in the record of the five-axis covariance of
the configuration-(a) central value. The status of that five-axis covariance and the scope of its use
are stated in §9.6. The choice of background treatment settles one of the conditions for using a
component; every other condition still applies.

D

Detector-response-mismatch closure test

The truth-shape and hidden-variable closures in §5 test recovery of deliberately modified event
populations. They do not directly test truth-distribution bias from a mismatch between the pseudodata detector response and OmniFold’s nominal response. Kevin McFarland suggested this distinction
and proposed stressing the hadronic record, especially for quasielastic events, to inspect low-W bias
(private communication). This appendix gives a validation protocol for such a test, not a result or

an uncertainty component. Its first stage, the reconstruction-only test below, has been implemented
but not yet run: no output of it has been produced and no pass threshold has been set. Closures of
the same kind, from the shifted response samples of Cresp , have been run (below).
Reconstruction-only test. The test uses the ordinary five-dimensional closure sample. After the
pseudo-data arrays are copied, and before background mixing or classifier training, it changes only
the reconstructed pseudo-data record
pseudo
pseudo
Eavail
−→ (1 + ϵ)Eavail
.

(32)

The nominal reconstructed simulation, all truth coordinates, event weights, and event membership
stay fixed. The pseudo-data therefore have a deliberately incompatible conditional response; OmniFold still uses the nominal response. Both signs of ϵ are required, at an amplitude fixed before
the results are examined. A documented hadronic-response variation or an explicit quantitative
assumption should justify that amplitude; the overall factor in the reconstructed-Eavail definition,
Eq. (2), is not by itself a sufficient basis.
Outputs include the full five-dimensional unfolded/reference residual, one-dimensional Eavail and
W projections, and an (Eavail , W ) ratio map. The primary observable is the signed bias versus W ,
with W < 1.1 GeV reported separately from the high-W tail. The global integral is a control rather
than the decision statistic. The implementation runs only as a pseudo-data closure and only on the
five observables, and cannot be combined with truth-reweight injection or bootstrapping. It labels
every output as a diagnostic that must not be quoted.
Closures from the shifted response samples. Noise-free, signal-only closures at the nominal
truth take pseudo-data from simulation whose reconstructed recoil response is shifted, either for
each deposit category of Cresp (§10.3) at its ±1σ or as a ±4 % scale of the whole recoil energy, and
unfold them with configuration (e) on the unshifted response. Each closure’s bias, less that of the
unshifted closure, is compared on the sixteen cells with the per-cell σ of Ctotal + Cresp . For the
deposit categories the median |∆|/σ is 0.07–0.20 and the largest 0.40; for the whole-recoil scale the
median is at most 0.50 and the largest 0.97. The closures are descriptive and carry no noise estimate:
no pass threshold is set, because the closure settings were deferred. They do not establish the bias
under the true detector response, validate Cresp or its category sizes, or describe response variants
other than those tested.
Physically correlated follow-up. The reconstruction-only test is by design a computationally
inexpensive adversarial stress test; it is not a detector model. A more faithful follow-up would select
quasielastic events, shift the leading-nucleon energy up and down, and recompute reconstructed
Eavail , q3 , W , and any affected selection from the altered low-level record. The current per-event
outputs hold only scalar observables, not that low-level record, so the follow-up requires dedicated
diagnostic output from the event processing. It is deferred unless the reconstruction-only test shows
material bias or the collaboration asks for the stronger response model.
Interpretation. The test leaves the quoted central value and the current covariance construction
unchanged. A small observed bias would support stability of the (Eavail , W ) interpretation only
within the tested response envelope. A large bias would require a decision on the response model or
an uncertainty component, and would not be absorbed silently into a non-closure error.

E

listed 2025 entries, with references and dimensionality: the number of kinematic variables unfolded
simultaneously in each analysis’s main differential result. The survey is nonexhaustive through 2026;
recent additions follow.
